for functions  $v_{t}:\mathbb{r}^{t + \ell}\to \mathbb{r}$  and  $u_{t + 1}:\mathbb{r}^{t + k}\to \mathbb{r}$ . in the first iteration  $t = 1$ , (5.3) is simply  $\mathbf{z}_1 = \mathbf{w}^\top \mathbf{u}_1$ . the debiasing coefficients  $a_{t1},\ldots ,a_{tt}$  and  $b_{t1},\ldots ,b_{t,t - 1}$  are defined to ensure that

$$
(\mathbf {y} _ {1}, \ldots , \mathbf {y} _ {t}) \stackrel {w} {\to} \mathcal {n} (0, \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {t} ^ {\infty}) \qquad \mathrm {a n d} \qquad (\mathbf {z} _ {1}, \ldots , \mathbf {z} _ {t}) \stackrel {w} {\to} \mathcal {n} (0, \boldsymbol {\omega} _ {t} ^ {\infty})
$$

as  $m, n \to \infty$ . we describe these debiasing coefficients and state evolution in the next section, in terms of the rectangular free cumulants of  $\mathbf{w}$ —these were also derived recently in [co20].

5.1. debiasing coefficients and limit covariance. define the  $t \times t$  matrices

$$
\begin{array}{l} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {t} = \left( \begin{array}{c c c c} \langle \mathbf {u} _ {1} ^ {2} \rangle & \langle \mathbf {u} _ {1} \mathbf {u} _ {2} \rangle & \dots & \langle \mathbf {u} _ {1} \mathbf {u} _ {t} \rangle \\ \langle \mathbf {u} _ {2} \mathbf {u} _ {1} \rangle & \langle \mathbf {u} _ {2} ^ {2} \rangle & \dots & \langle \mathbf {u} _ {2} \mathbf {u} _ {t} \rangle \\ \vdots & \vdots & \ddots & \vdots \\ \langle \mathbf {u} _ {t} \mathbf {u} _ {1} \rangle & \langle \mathbf {u} _ {t} \mathbf {u} _ {2} \rangle & \dots & \langle \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {2} \rangle \end{array} \right), \quad \boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} = \left( \begin{array}{c c c c c} 0 & 0 & \dots & 0 & 0 \\ \langle \partial_ {1} \mathbf {u} _ {2} \rangle & 0 & \dots & 0 & 0 \\ \langle \partial_ {1} \mathbf {u} _ {3} \rangle & \langle \partial_ {2} \mathbf {u} _ {3} \rangle & \dots & 0 & 0 \\ \vdots & \vdots & \ddots & \vdots & \vdots \\ \langle \partial_ {1} \mathbf {u} _ {t} \rangle & \langle \partial_ {2} \mathbf {u} _ {t} \rangle & \dots & \langle \partial_ {t - 1} \mathbf {u} _ {t} \rangle & 0 \end{array} \right), \tag {5.7} \\ \boldsymbol {\gamma} _ {t} = \left( \begin{array}{c c c c} \langle \mathbf {v} _ {1} ^ {2} \rangle & \langle \mathbf {v} _ {1} \mathbf {v} _ {2} \rangle & \dots & \langle \mathbf {v} _ {1} \mathbf {v} _ {t} \rangle \\ \langle \mathbf {v} _ {2} \mathbf {v} _ {1} \rangle & \langle \mathbf {v} _ {2} ^ {2} \rangle & \dots & \langle \mathbf {v} _ {2} \mathbf {v} _ {t} \rangle \\ \vdots & \vdots & \ddots & \vdots \\ \langle \mathbf {v} _ {t} \mathbf {v} _ {1} \rangle & \langle \mathbf {v} _ {t} \mathbf {v} _ {2} \rangle & \dots & \langle \mathbf {v} _ {t} ^ {2} \rangle \end{array} \right), \quad \boldsymbol {\psi} _ {t} = \left( \begin{array}{c c c c} \langle \partial_ {1} \mathbf {v} _ {1} \rangle & 0 & \dots & 0 \\ \langle \partial_ {1} \mathbf {v} _ {2} \rangle & \langle \partial_ {2} \mathbf {v} _ {2} \rangle & \dots & 0 \\ \vdots & \vdots & \ddots & \vdots \\ \langle \partial_ {1} \mathbf {v} _ {t} \rangle & \langle \partial_ {2} \mathbf {v} _ {t} \rangle & \dots & \langle \partial_ {t} \mathbf {v} _ {t} \rangle \end{array} \right). \\ \end{array}
$$

for each  $j\geq 0$  , define

$$
\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {t} ^ {(j)} = \sum_ {i = 0} ^ {j} \left(\boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} \boldsymbol {\psi} _ {t}\right) ^ {i} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {t} \left(\boldsymbol {\psi} _ {t} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} ^ {\top}\right) ^ {j - i} + \sum_ {i = 0} ^ {j - 1} \left(\boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} \boldsymbol {\psi} _ {t}\right) ^ {i} \boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} \boldsymbol {\gamma} _ {t} \boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} ^ {\top} \left(\boldsymbol {\psi} _ {t} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} ^ {\top}\right) ^ {j - 1 - i}, \tag {5.9}
$$

$$
\boldsymbol {\xi} _ {t} ^ {(j)} = \sum_ {i = 0} ^ {j} \left(\boldsymbol {\psi} _ {t} \boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t}\right) ^ {i} \boldsymbol {\gamma} _ {t} \left(\boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\psi} _ {t} ^ {\top}\right) ^ {j - i} + \sum_ {i = 0} ^ {j - 1} \left(\boldsymbol {\psi} _ {t} \boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t}\right) ^ {i} \boldsymbol {\psi} _ {t} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {t} \boldsymbol {\psi} _ {t} ^ {\top} \left(\boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\psi} _ {t} ^ {\top}\right) ^ {j - 1 - i}. \tag {5.10}
$$

the second summations of (5.9) and (5.10) are not present for  $j = 0$ . so for example,

$$
\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {t} ^ {(0)} = \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {t}
$$

$$
\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {t} ^ {(1)} = \boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} \boldsymbol {\psi} _ {t} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {t} + \boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} \boldsymbol {\gamma} _ {t} \boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} ^ {\top} + \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {t} \boldsymbol {\psi} _ {t} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} ^ {\top}
$$

$$
\begin{array}{l} \theta_ {t} ^ {(2)} = \phi_ {t} \psi_ {t} \phi_ {t} \psi_ {t} \delta_ {t} + \phi_ {t} \psi_ {t} \phi_ {t} \gamma_ {t} \phi_ {t} ^ {\top} + \phi_ {t} \psi_ {t} \delta_ {t} \psi_ {t} ^ {\top} \phi_ {t} ^ {\top} \\ + \phi_ {t} \gamma_ {t} \phi_ {t} ^ {\top} \psi_ {t} ^ {\top} \phi_ {t} ^ {\top} + \delta_ {t} \psi_ {t} ^ {\top} \phi_ {t} ^ {\top} \psi_ {t} ^ {\top} \phi_ {t} ^ {\top} \\ \end{array}
$$

$$
\boldsymbol {\xi} _ {t} ^ {(0)} = \boldsymbol {\gamma} _ {t}
$$

$$
\boldsymbol {\xi} _ {t} ^ {(1)} = \boldsymbol {\psi} _ {t} \boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} \boldsymbol {\gamma} _ {t} + \boldsymbol {\psi} _ {t} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {t} \boldsymbol {\psi} _ {t} ^ {\top} + \boldsymbol {\gamma} _ {t} \boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\psi} _ {t} ^ {\top}
$$

$$
\begin{array}{l} \boldsymbol {\xi} _ {t} ^ {(2)} = \boldsymbol {\psi} _ {t} \boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} \boldsymbol {\psi} _ {t} \boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} \boldsymbol {\gamma} _ {t} + \boldsymbol {\psi} _ {t} \boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} \boldsymbol {\psi} _ {t} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {t} \boldsymbol {\psi} _ {t} ^ {\top} + \boldsymbol {\psi} _ {t} \boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} \boldsymbol {\gamma} _ {t} \boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\psi} _ {t} ^ {\top} \\ + \psi_ {t} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {t} \psi_ {t} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} ^ {\top} \psi_ {t} ^ {\top} + \gamma_ {t} \boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} ^ {\top} \psi_ {t} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} ^ {\top} \psi_ {t} ^ {\top}. \\ \end{array}
$$

let  $\{\kappa_{2k}\}_{k\geq 1}$  be the rectangular free cumulants with aspect ratio  $\gamma = m / n$  corresponding to the sequence of even moments

$$
m _ {2 k} = \frac {1}{m} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {\min  (m, n)} \lambda_ {i} ^ {2 k}, \tag {5.11}
$$

as defined in section 2.4. note that we always use the normalization  $1 / m$ , so these are the moments of  $\pmb{\lambda}$  padded by  $m - n$  additional 0's if  $m > n$ .

define the  $t\times t$  matrices

$$
\mathbf {a} _ {t} = \left(\sum_ {j = 0} ^ {\infty} \kappa_ {2 (j + 1)} \boldsymbol {\psi} _ {t} \left(\boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} \boldsymbol {\psi} _ {t}\right) ^ {j}\right) ^ {\top}, \quad \mathbf {b} _ {t} = \left(\gamma \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {\infty} \kappa_ {2 (j + 1)} \boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} \left(\boldsymbol {\psi} _ {t} \boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t}\right) ^ {j}\right) ^ {\top}, \tag {5.12}
$$

$$
\boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {t} = \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {\infty} \kappa_ {2 (j + 1)} \boldsymbol {\xi} _ {t} ^ {(j)}, \quad \boldsymbol {\omega} _ {t} = \gamma \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {\infty} \kappa_ {2 (j + 1)} \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {t} ^ {(j)}. \tag {5.13}
$$

these are in fact finite series, as it may be verified that

$$
\boldsymbol {\psi} _ {t} (\boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} \boldsymbol {\psi} _ {t}) ^ {j} = 0 \text {f o r} j \geq t + 1
$$

$$
\begin{array}{l} \phi_ {t} \left(\psi_ {t} \phi_ {t}\right) ^ {j} = 0 \text {f o r} j \geq t \\ \boldsymbol {\xi} _ {t} ^ {(j)} = 0 \text {f o r} j \geq 2 t \\ \theta_ {t} ^ {(j)} = 0 \text {f o r} j \geq 2 t - 1. \\ \end{array}
$$

so for example,

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbf {a} _ {1} = \kappa_ {2} \boldsymbol {\psi} _ {1} ^ {\top}, \qquad \mathbf {a} _ {2} = \kappa_ {2} \boldsymbol {\psi} _ {2} ^ {\top} + \kappa_ {4} \big (\boldsymbol {\psi} _ {2} \boldsymbol {\phi} _ {2} \boldsymbol {\psi} _ {2} \big) ^ {\top}, \quad \ldots . \\ \mathbf {b} _ {1} = 0, \quad \mathbf {b} _ {2} = \gamma \kappa_ {2} \boldsymbol {\phi} _ {2} ^ {\top}, \quad \mathbf {b} _ {3} = \gamma \kappa_ {2} \boldsymbol {\phi} _ {3} ^ {\top} + \gamma \kappa_ {4} (\boldsymbol {\phi} _ {3} \boldsymbol {\psi} _ {3} \boldsymbol {\phi} _ {3}) ^ {\top}, \quad \dots \\ \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {1} = \kappa_ {2} \boldsymbol {\xi} _ {1} ^ {(0)} + \kappa_ {4} \boldsymbol {\xi} _ {1} ^ {(1)}, \qquad \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {2} = \kappa_ {2} \boldsymbol {\xi} _ {2} ^ {(0)} + \kappa_ {4} \boldsymbol {\xi} _ {2} ^ {(1)} + \kappa_ {6} \boldsymbol {\xi} _ {2} ^ {(2)} + \kappa_ {8} \boldsymbol {\xi} _ {2} ^ {(3)}, \quad \ldots \\ \boldsymbol {\omega} _ {1} = \gamma \kappa_ {2} \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {1} ^ {(0)}, \qquad \boldsymbol {\omega} _ {2} = \gamma \kappa_ {2} \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {2} ^ {(0)} + \gamma \kappa_ {4} \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {2} ^ {(1)} + \gamma \kappa_ {6} \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {2} ^ {(2)}, \quad \ldots \\ \end{array}
$$

the matrices  $\mathbf{a}_t$  and  $\mathbf{b}_t$  are upper-triangular, with the forms

$$
\mathbf {a} _ {t} = \left( \begin{array}{c c c c} a _ {1 1} & a _ {2 1} & \dots & a _ {t 1} \\ & a _ {2 2} & \dots & a _ {t 2} \\ & & \ddots & \vdots \\ & & & a _ {t t} \end{array} \right), \qquad \mathbf {b} _ {t} = \left( \begin{array}{c c c c c} 0 & b _ {2 1} & b _ {3 1} & \dots & b _ {t 1} \\ & 0 & b _ {3 2} & \dots & b _ {t 2} \\ & & \ddots & \ddots & \vdots \\ & & & 0 & b _ {t, t - 1} \\ & & & & 0 \end{array} \right).
$$

the debiasing coefficients  $a_{t1}, \ldots, a_{tt}, b_{t1}, \ldots, b_{t,t-1}$  in (5.3) and (5.5) are defined as the last columns of  $\mathbf{a}_t$  and  $\mathbf{b}_t$ . under the conditions to be imposed in assumption 5.2, these matrices all have deterministic  $t \times t$  limits

$$
(\pmb {\delta} _ {t} ^ {\infty}, \pmb {\gamma} _ {t} ^ {\infty}, \pmb {\phi} _ {t} ^ {\infty}, \pmb {\psi} _ {t} ^ {\infty}, \mathbf {a} _ {t} ^ {\infty}, \mathbf {b} _ {t} ^ {\infty}, \pmb {\sigma} _ {t} ^ {\infty}, \pmb {\omega} _ {t} ^ {\infty}) = \lim _ {m, n \to \infty} (\pmb {\delta} _ {t}, \pmb {\gamma} _ {t}, \pmb {\phi} _ {t}, \pmb {\psi} _ {t}, \mathbf {a} _ {t}, \mathbf {b} _ {t}, \pmb {\sigma} _ {t}, \pmb {\omega} _ {t}).
$$

the matrices  $\pmb{\sigma}_t^\infty$  and  $\pmb{\omega}_t^\infty$  are the covariances in the state evolutions for  $(\mathbf{y}_1, \dots, \mathbf{y}_t)$  and  $(\mathbf{z}_1, \dots, \mathbf{z}_t)$ . as in the symmetric square setting, the debiasing coefficients in (5.3) and (5.5) may be replaced by their limits  $a_{ts}^\infty$  and  $b_{ts}^\infty$ , or by any consistent estimates of these limits.

we make the following observations about the above definitions:

(1) the upper-left  $(t - 1)\times (t - 1)$  submatrices of  $\mathbf{a}_t,\mathbf{b}_t,\pmb {\sigma}_t,\pmb {\omega}_t$  coincide with the matrices  $\mathbf{a}_{t - 1},\mathbf{b}_{t - 1},\pmb{\sigma}_{t - 1},\pmb{\omega}_{t - 1}$  
(2) for each  $t \geq 1$ ,  $\mathbf{a}_t, \mathbf{b}_t, \boldsymbol{\sigma}_t, \boldsymbol{\omega}_t$  depend respectively only on the rectangular free cumulants of  $\lambda$  up to the orders  $\kappa_{2t}, \kappa_{2t-2}, \kappa_{4t}, \kappa_{4t-2}$ .  
(3) the matrices  $\mathbf{a}_t, \mathbf{\sigma}_t$  depend on  $\mathbf{u}_1, \ldots, \mathbf{u}_t, \mathbf{v}_1, \ldots, \mathbf{v}_t$  and their derivatives. the matrices  $\mathbf{b}_t, \mathbf{\omega}_t$  depend on  $\mathbf{u}_1, \ldots, \mathbf{u}_t, \mathbf{v}_1, \ldots, \mathbf{v}_{t-1}$  and their derivatives, but they do not depend on  $\mathbf{v}_t$  or its derivatives. (thus the debiasing coefficients and state evolution for  $\mathbf{z}_t$  in (5.3) are well-defined before defining  $\mathbf{v}_t$  in (5.4).)

the first two statements are analogous to our observations in the symmetric square setting. the third statement holds from the definitions of  $\mathbf{b}_t$  and  $\omega_t$  in (5.12-5.13), because the last column of  $\phi_t$  is 0, so  $\phi_t\psi_t$  does not depend on the last row of  $\psi_t$ , and  $\phi_t\gamma_t\phi_t^\top$  does not depend on the last row or column of  $\gamma_t$ .

remark 5.1. in the gaussian setting where  $\mathbf{w}$  has i.i.d.  $\mathcal{n}(0,1/n)$  entries, the limit spectral distribution of  $\mathbf{ww}^{\top}$  is the marcenko-pastur law, with limiting rectangular free cumulants

$$
\kappa_ {2} ^ {\infty} = 1, \quad \kappa_ {2 j} ^ {\infty} = 0 \quad \text {f o r a l l} j \geq 2.
$$

this yields simply

$$
\mathbf {a} _ {t} ^ {\infty} = \left(\boldsymbol {\psi} _ {t} ^ {\infty}\right) ^ {\top}, \qquad \mathbf {b} _ {t} ^ {\infty} = \gamma \left(\boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} ^ {\infty}\right) ^ {\top}, \qquad \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {t} ^ {\infty} = \boldsymbol {\gamma} _ {t} ^ {\infty}, \qquad \boldsymbol {\omega} _ {t} = \gamma \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {t} ^ {\infty}.
$$

if we further specialize to an algorithm where  $v_{t}$  depends only on  $z_{t}$  and  $u_{t + 1}$  depends only on  $y_{t}$ , then  $\langle \partial_s\mathbf{u}_t\rangle = 0$  for all  $s\neq t - 1$  and  $\langle \partial_s\mathbf{z}_t\rangle = 0$  for all  $s\neq t$ . this yields the gaussian amp algorithm

$$
\mathbf {z} _ {t} = \mathbf {w} ^ {\top} \mathbf {u} _ {t} - \gamma \langle \partial_ {t - 1} \mathbf {u} _ {t} \rangle \mathbf {v} _ {t - 1}
$$

$$
\mathbf {v} _ {t} = v _ {t} (\mathbf {z} _ {t}, \mathbf {f})
$$

$$
\mathbf {y} _ {t} = \mathbf {w v} _ {t} - \langle \partial_ {t} \mathbf {v} _ {t} \rangle \mathbf {u} _ {t}
$$

$$
\mathbf {u} _ {t + 1} = u _ {t + 1} (\mathbf {y} _ {t}, \mathbf {e})
$$

as studied in [bm11a, section 3]. furthermore, the state evolution is such that  $\mathbf{z}_t$  has the empirical limit  $\mathcal{n}(0,\omega_{tt}^{\infty})$  where  $\omega_{tt}^{\infty} = \lim_{m,n\to \infty}\gamma \cdot \langle \mathbf{u}_t^2\rangle$ , and  $\mathbf{y}_t$  has the empirical limit  $\mathcal{n}(0,\sigma_{tt}^{\infty})$  where  $\sigma_{tt}^{\infty} = \lim_{m,n\to \infty}\langle \mathbf{v}_t^2\rangle$ .

note that outside of this gaussian setting, in general we do not have the identities  $\pmb{\sigma}_t = \pmb{\gamma}_t$  and  $\pmb{\omega}_t = \gamma \pmb{\delta}_t$  even when  $\mathbf{w}$  is normalized such that  $\kappa_2 = 1$ .

5.2. main result. we impose the following assumptions on the model (5.1-5.2) and the amp iterates (5.3-5.6). again, we do not require here  $v_{t}(\cdot)$  and  $u_{t + 1}(\cdot)$  to be lipschitz.

# assumption 5.2.

(a)  $m,n\to \infty$  such that  $m / n = \gamma \in (0,\infty)$  is a fixed constant.  
(b)  $\mathbf{o} \in \mathbb{r}^{m \times m}$  and  $\mathbf{q} \in \mathbb{r}^{n \times n}$  are independent random and haar-uniform orthogonal matrices.  
(c)  $\pmb{\lambda} \in \mathbb{r}^{\min(m,n)}$  is independent of  $\mathbf{o}, \mathbf{q}$  and satisfies  $\pmb{\lambda} \xrightarrow{w} \pmb{\lambda}$  almost surely as  $m, n \to \infty$ , for a random variable  $\pmb{\lambda}$  having finite moments of all orders.  
(d)  $\mathbf{u}_1\in \mathbb{r}^m$ $\mathbf{e}\in \mathbb{r}^{m\times k}$ , and  $\mathbf{f}\in \mathbb{r}^{n\times \ell}$  are independent of  $\mathbf{o},\mathbf{q}$  and satisfy  $(\mathbf{u}_1,\mathbf{e})\stackrel {w}{\to}(u_1,e)$  and  $\mathbf{f}\stackrel {w}{\to}f$  almost surely as  $m,n\to \infty$ , where  $(u_{1},e)\equiv (u_{1},e_{1},\ldots ,e_{k})$  and  $f\equiv (f_{1},\dots ,f_{\ell})$  are random vectors having finite moments of all orders.  
(e) each function  $v_{t}:\mathbb{r}^{t + \ell}\to \mathbb{r}$  and  $u_{t + 1}:\mathbb{r}^{t + k}\to \mathbb{r}$  satisfies (2.1) for some  $c > 0$  and  $p\geq 1$ . writing their arguments as  $(z,f)$  and  $(y,e)$  where  $z,y\in \mathbb{r}^t$ ,  $f\in \mathbb{r}^{\ell}$ , and  $e\in \mathbb{r}^k$ ,  $v_{t}$  is weakly differentiable in  $z$  and continuous in  $f$ , and  $u_{t + 1}$  is weakly differentiable in  $y$  and continuous in  $e$ . for each  $s = 1,\ldots ,t$ ,  $\partial_{s}v_{t}$  and  $\partial_{s}u_{t + 1}$  also satisfy (2.1) for some  $c > 0$  and  $p\geq 1$ , where  $\partial_s v_t(z,f)$  is continuous at lebesgue-a.e.  $z\in \mathbb{r}^t$  for every  $f\in \mathbb{r}^{\ell}$ , and  $\partial_{s}u_{t + 1}(y,e)$  is continuous at lebesgue-a.e.  $y\in \mathbb{r}^t$  for every  $e\in \mathbb{r}^k$ .  
(f)  $\operatorname{var}[\lambda] > 0$  and  $\mathbb{e}[u_1^2] > 0$ . letting  $(z_1, \ldots, z_t) \sim \mathcal{n}(0, \omega_t^\infty)$  be independent of  $f$ , there do not exist constants  $\alpha_1, \ldots, \alpha_t, \beta_1, \ldots, \beta_{t-1}$  for which

$$
v _ {t} (z _ {1}, \dots , z _ {t}, f) = \sum_ {s = 1} ^ {t} \alpha_ {s} z _ {s} + \sum_ {s = 1} ^ {t - 1} \beta_ {s} v _ {s} (z _ {1}, \dots , z _ {s}, f)
$$

with probability 1 over  $(f,z_1,\ldots ,z_t)$ . letting  $(y_{1},\dots ,y_{t})\sim \mathcal{n}(0,\pmb{\sigma}_{t}^{\infty})$  be independent of  $(u_{1},e)$ , there do not exist constants  $\alpha_{1},\ldots ,\alpha_{t},\beta_{1},\ldots ,\beta_{t}$  for which

$$
u _ {t + 1} (y _ {1}, \dots , y _ {t}, e) = \sum_ {s = 1} ^ {t} \alpha_ {s} y _ {s} + \beta_ {1} u _ {1} + \sum_ {s = 2} ^ {t} \beta_ {s} u _ {s} (y _ {1}, \dots , y _ {s - 1}, e)
$$

with probability 1 over  $(u_{1},e,y_{1},\ldots ,y_{t})$

as in the symmetric square setting, we clarify that theorem 5.3 below establishes the existence of  $\omega_t^\infty$  when condition (f) holds for  $u_1, \ldots, u_t$  and  $v_1, \ldots, v_{t-1}$ , and this limit  $\omega_t^\infty$  then defines condition (f) for  $v_t$ . similarly, the theorem establishes the existence of  $\sigma_t^\infty$  when condition (f) holds for  $u_1, \ldots, u_t$  and  $v_1, \ldots, v_t$ , and this limit  $\sigma_t^\infty$  then defines the condition for  $u_{t+1}$ . this condition (f) is a non-degeneracy assumption that will hold as long as  $u_{t+1}(\cdot)$  and  $v_t(\cdot)$  depend non-linearly on  $y_t$  and  $z_t$ , respectively.

theorem 5.3. under assumption 5.2, for each fixed  $t \geq 1$ , almost surely as  $n \to \infty$ :  $\pmb{\sigma}_{t} \rightarrow \pmb{\sigma}_{t}^{\infty}$  and  $\omega_{t} \rightarrow \omega_{t}^{\infty}$  for some deterministic non-singular matrices  $\pmb{\sigma}_{t}^{\infty}$  and  $\pmb{\omega}_{t}^{\infty}$ . also,

$$
\left(\mathbf {u} _ {1}, \dots , \mathbf {u} _ {t + 1}, \mathbf {y} _ {1}, \dots , \mathbf {y} _ {t}, \mathbf {e}\right) \stackrel {{w}} {{\rightarrow}} \left(u _ {1}, \dots , u _ {t + 1}, y _ {1}, \dots , y _ {t}, e\right)
$$

$$
\left(\mathbf {v} _ {1}, \dots , \mathbf {v} _ {t}, \mathbf {z} _ {1}, \dots , \mathbf {z} _ {t}, \mathbf {f}\right) \stackrel {{w}} {{\to}} \left(v _ {1}, \dots , v _ {t}, z _ {1}, \dots , z _ {t}, f\right)
$$

where  $(y_{1},\ldots ,y_{t})\sim \mathcal{n}(0,\pmb{\sigma}_{t}^{\infty})$  is independent of  $(u_{1},e)$ ;  $(z_{1},\dots,z_{t})\sim \mathcal{n}(0,\pmb{\omega}_{t}^{\infty})$  is independent of  $f$ ;  $u_{s} = u_{s}(z_{1},\dots,z_{s - 1},e)$  for each  $s = 2,\dots,t + 1$ ; and  $v_{s} = v_{s}(z_{1},\dots,z_{s},f)$  for each  $s = 1,\dots,t$ .

the limits  $\pmb{\sigma}_{t}^{\infty}$  and  $\pmb{\omega}_{t}^{\infty}$  are given by replacing  $\langle \mathbf{u}_s\mathbf{u}_{s'}\rangle$ ,  $\langle \mathbf{v}_s\mathbf{v}_{s'}\rangle$ ,  $\langle \partial_{s'}\mathbf{u}_s\rangle$ ,  $\langle \partial_{s'}\mathbf{v}_s\rangle$ , and  $\kappa_{2k}$  in the definitions (5.7-5.8) and (5.13) with  $\mathbb{e}[u_su_{s'}]$ ,  $\mathbb{e}[v_sv_{s'}]$ ,  $\mathbb{e}[\partial_{s'}u_s(y_1,\dots,y_{s - 1},e)]$ ,  $\mathbb{e}[\partial_{s'}v_s(z_1,\dots,z_s,f)]$ , and  $\kappa_{2k}^{\infty}$ .

the proof of this result is provided in appendix b. as in corollary 4.4, we may remove the non-degeneracy condition in assumption 5.2(f) if  $v_{t}$  and  $u_{t+1}$  are continuously-differentiable and lipschitz. this is stated in the following corollary. the proof follows the same argument as that of corollary 4.4, and we omit this for brevity.

corollary 5.4. suppose assumption 5.2(a-d) holds,  $\lim \sup_{n\to \infty}\| \pmb {\lambda}\|_{\infty} < \infty$ , each function  $v_{t}$ :  $\mathbb{r}^{t + \ell}\rightarrow \mathbb{r}$  and  $u_{t + 1}:\mathbb{r}^{t + k}\rightarrow \mathbb{r}$  is continuously-differentiable, and

$$
| v _ {t} (z, f) - v _ {t} (z ^ {\prime}, f) | \leq c \| z - z ^ {\prime} \|, \qquad | u _ {t + 1} (y, e) - u _ {t + 1} (y ^ {\prime}, e) | \leq c \| y - y ^ {\prime} \|
$$

for a constant  $c > 0$  and all  $z, z', y, y' \in \mathbb{r}^t$ ,  $e \in \mathbb{r}^k$ , and  $f \in \mathbb{r}^\ell$ . then for each fixed  $t \geq 1$ , almost surely as  $n \to \infty$ :  $\pmb{\sigma}_t \to \pmb{\sigma}_t^\infty$  and  $\omega_t \to \omega_t^\infty$  for some deterministic (possibly singular) matrices  $\pmb{\sigma}_t^\infty$  and  $\omega_t^\infty$ , and

$$
\left(\mathbf {u} _ {1}, \dots , \mathbf {u} _ {t + 1}, \mathbf {y} _ {1}, \dots , \mathbf {y} _ {t}, \mathbf {e}\right) \stackrel {{w _ {2}}} {{\rightarrow}} \left(u _ {1}, \dots , u _ {t + 1}, y _ {1}, \dots , y _ {t}, e\right)
$$

$$
\left(\mathbf {v} _ {1}, \ldots , \mathbf {v} _ {t}, \mathbf {z} _ {1}, \ldots , \mathbf {z} _ {t}, \mathbf {f}\right) \stackrel {w _ {2}} {\rightarrow} \left(v _ {1}, \ldots , v _ {t}, z _ {1}, \ldots , z _ {t}, f\right)
$$

where these limits are as defined in theorem 5.3.

# 6. proof ideas

we describe here the main ideas of the proofs. in the setting of a symmetric square matrix  $\mathbf{w} \in \mathbb{r}^{n \times n}$ , the basic strategy is to write  $\mathbf{w} = \mathbf{o}^{\top} \boldsymbol{\lambda} \mathbf{o}$ , and to express the amp iterations (1.2-1.3) in an expanded form as

$$
\mathbf {r} _ {t} = \mathbf {o u} _ {t} \tag {6.1}
$$

$$
\mathbf {s} _ {t} = \mathbf {o} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\lambda} \mathbf {r} _ {t} \tag {6.2}
$$

$$
\mathbf {z} _ {t} = \mathbf {s} _ {t} - b _ {t 1} \mathbf {u} _ {1} - \dots - b _ {t t} \mathbf {u} _ {t} \tag {6.3}
$$

$$
\mathbf {u} _ {t + 1} = u _ {t + 1} \left(\mathbf {z} _ {1}, \dots , \mathbf {z} _ {t}\right). \tag {6.4}
$$

all analyses are performed conditional on  $\mathbf{u}_1$  and  $\pmb{\lambda}$ , so that the only randomness is in the haar-orthogonal matrix  $\mathbf{o}$ . we apply bolthausen's conditioning technique [bol14], analyzing sequentially each iterate  $\mathbf{r}_1, \mathbf{s}_1, \mathbf{z}_1, \mathbf{u}_2, \mathbf{r}_2, \ldots$  conditional on all preceding iterates. this requires understanding the law of  $\mathbf{o}$  conditional on events of the form

$$
\mathbf {o x} = \mathbf {y},
$$

which was shown in [rsf19, tak17] to be

$$
\mathbf {o} | _ {\mathbf {o x} = \mathbf {y}} \stackrel {l} {=} \mathbf {x} \left(\mathbf {x} ^ {\top} \mathbf {x}\right) ^ {- 1} \mathbf {y} ^ {\top} + \pi_ {\mathbf {x} ^ {\perp}} \tilde {\mathbf {o}} \pi_ {\mathbf {y} ^ {\perp}} ^ {\top}. \tag {6.5}
$$

here,  $\pi_{\mathbf{x}^{\perp}}$  and  $\pi_{\mathbf{y}^{\perp}}$  are matrices with orthonormal columns spanning the orthogonal complements of the column spans of  $\mathbf{x}$  and  $\mathbf{y}$ , and  $\tilde{\mathbf{o}}$  is an independent haar-orthogonal matrix. applying (6.5) to the appearances of  $\mathbf{o}$  in (6.1-6.2), we will exhibit decompositions

$$
\mathbf {r} _ {t} = \mathbf {r} _ {| |} + \mathbf {r} _ {\perp}, \qquad \mathbf {s} _ {t} = \mathbf {s} _ {| |} + \mathbf {s} _ {\perp}.
$$

the vectors  $\mathbf{r}_{\perp}$  and  $\mathbf{s}_{\perp}$  arise from the second term of (6.5) and have empirical distributions that are approximately gaussian conditional on the preceding iterates. the vectors  $\mathbf{r}_{\parallel}$  and  $\mathbf{s}_{\parallel}$  arise from the first term of (6.5), are deterministic conditional on the preceding iterates, and represent biases respectively in the directions of  $(\mathbf{r}_1,\dots ,\mathbf{r}_{t - 1},\boldsymbol {\lambda}\mathbf{r}_{t - 1},\dots ,\boldsymbol {\lambda}\mathbf{r}_{t - 1})$  and  $(\mathbf{u}_1,\dots ,\mathbf{u}_t,\mathbf{z}_1,\dots ,\mathbf{z}_{t - 1})$ . the onsager correction by  $b_{t1}\mathbf{u}_1 + \ldots +b_{tt}\mathbf{u}_t$  in (6.3) is defined to exactly cancel the component of this bias  $\mathbf{s}_{\parallel}$  in  $(\mathbf{u}_1,\dots ,\mathbf{u}_t)$ , so that  $(\mathbf{z}_1,\dots ,\mathbf{z}_t)$  has an approximate joint gaussian law. when the spectrum of  $\mathbf{w}$  converges to wigner's semicircle law, the forms of  $\mathbf{r}_{\parallel}$  and  $\mathbf{s}_{\parallel}$  and variances of  $\mathbf{r}_{\perp}$  and  $\mathbf{s}_{\perp}$  are more straightforward to track across iterations, and this produces a slightly different proof of the amp analyses in [bm11a, bol14].

when the spectrum of  $\mathbf{w}$  does not converge to the semicircle law, two difficulties arise in carrying out this conditional analysis. first, the forms of  $\mathbf{r}_{\parallel}, \mathbf{r}_{\perp}, \mathbf{s}_{\parallel}, \mathbf{s}_{\perp}$  in iteration  $t$  will depend on

$$
n ^ {- 1} \mathbf {u} _ {s} ^ {\top} \mathbf {w} ^ {k} \mathbf {u} _ {t} \equiv n ^ {- 1} \mathbf {r} _ {s} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\lambda} ^ {k} \mathbf {r} _ {t} \qquad \mathrm {f o r} k = 1, 2 \mathrm {a n d} s, t \leq t.
$$

these values will in turn depend on

$$
n ^ {- 1} \mathbf {u} _ {s} ^ {\top} \mathbf {w} ^ {k} \mathbf {u} _ {t} \equiv n ^ {- 1} \mathbf {r} _ {s} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\lambda} ^ {k} \mathbf {r} _ {t} \qquad \mathrm {f o r} k = 1, \ldots , 4 \mathrm {a n d} s, t \leq t - 1,
$$

which will in turn depend on

$$
n ^ {- 1} \mathbf {u} _ {s} ^ {\top} \mathbf {w} ^ {k} \mathbf {u} _ {t} \equiv n ^ {- 1} \mathbf {r} _ {s} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\lambda} ^ {k} \mathbf {r} _ {t} \qquad \mathrm {f o r} k = 1, \ldots , 6 \mathrm {a n d} s, t \leq t - 2,
$$

and so forth. the final dependence is on  $n^{-1}\mathbf{u}_1^\top \mathbf{w}^k\mathbf{u}_1$  for  $k = 1,\dots ,2t$ , whose large-  $n$  limits are given by the first  $2t$  moments of the limit spectral distribution of  $\mathbf{w}$ , because the initialization  $\mathbf{u}_1$  is independent of  $\mathbf{w}$  which is rotationally invariant in law. the free cumulants of  $\mathbf{w}$  that appear in the final forms of the onsager correction and state evolution emerge by tracking these dependences. to provide an inductive argument that can describe these dependences for arbitrary iterations, our proof establishes a precise form of

$$
\lim _ {n \to \infty} n ^ {- 1} \mathbf {u} _ {s} ^ {\top} \mathbf {w} ^ {k} \mathbf {u} _ {t}
$$

for every fixed moment  $k \geq 0$  and all fixed iterates  $s, t \geq 1$ . these forms depend on combinatorial coefficients that we call "partial moment coefficients", defined by summing over certain subsets of the non-crossing partition lattice, and which interpolate between the moments and free cumulants of the spectral distribution of  $\mathbf{w}$ . we define these coefficients in appendix a.1.

a second technical difficulty which arises is that for the resulting conditioning events  $\mathbf{o}\mathbf{x} = \mathbf{y}$ , the form of the matrix  $\mathbf{x}^{\top}\mathbf{x}$  in (6.5) becomes complicated, depending on series of matrices with these partial moment coefficients, and  $(\mathbf{x}^{\top}\mathbf{x})^{-1}$  does not admit a tractable description. instead, we handle matrix-vector products  $(\mathbf{x}^{\top}\mathbf{x})^{-1}\mathbf{v}$  arising in the computation by "guessing" the form  $\mathbf{w}$  for this product, and then verifying that  $(\mathbf{x}^{\top}\mathbf{x})\mathbf{w} = \mathbf{v}$ . this type of verification is contained in lemma a.3, and relies on combinatorial identities for these partial moment coefficients.

the proof ideas in the rectangular setting are similar: we write  $\mathbf{w} = \mathbf{o}^{\top}\mathbf{\lambda}\mathbf{q}$  and express (1.5- 1.8) in an expanded form analogous to (6.1-6.4) above. a key component of the proof is then to identify the large-  $(m,n)$  limits of the four quantities

$$
m ^ {- 1} \mathbf {u} _ {s} ^ {\top} (\mathbf {w} \mathbf {w} ^ {\top}) ^ {k} \mathbf {u} _ {t}, \quad m ^ {- 1} \mathbf {v} _ {s} ^ {\top} \mathbf {w} ^ {\top} (\mathbf {w} \mathbf {w} ^ {\top}) ^ {k} \mathbf {u} _ {t},
$$

$$
n ^ {- 1} \mathbf {u} _ {s} ^ {\top} \mathbf {w} (\mathbf {w} ^ {\top} \mathbf {w}) ^ {k} \mathbf {v} _ {t}, \quad n ^ {- 1} \mathbf {v} _ {s} ^ {\top} (\mathbf {w} ^ {\top} \mathbf {w}) ^ {k} \mathbf {v} _ {t}
$$

for all fixed moments  $k \geq 0$  and iterates  $s, t \geq 1$ . these will depend on certain partial moment coefficients that interpolate between the moments and rectangular free cumulants of the limit singular value distribution of  $\mathbf{w}$ , and which are defined by summing over subsets of the lattice of

non-crossing partitions of sets with even cardinality. these coefficients are defined in appendix b.1, and the corresponding identities involving  $(\mathbf{x}^{\top}\mathbf{x})^{-1}$  are contained in lemma b.3.

for the analyses of the bayes-amp algorithms for pca in section 3, part (a) of theorems 3.1 and 3.4 are straightforward consequences of the results for the general amp algorithms. part (b) of these theorems require an analysis of the state evolutions for the single-iterate posterior mean denoisers, which we carry out in appendix c.2. this analysis applies a contractive mapping argument to show that for sufficiently large signal strengths, the matrices  $\pmb{\delta}_t$ ,  $\pmb{\sigma}_t$ ,  $\pmb{\gamma}_t$ , and  $\pmb{\omega}_t$  all converge as  $t \to \infty$  in a space of "infinite matrices" equipped with a weighted  $\ell_{\infty}$  metric.

# appendix a. proof for symmetric square matrices

in this appendix, we prove theorem 4.3. recalling  $\mathbf{w} = \mathbf{o}^{\top}\pmb{\lambda}\mathbf{o}$  where  $\pmb{\lambda} = \mathrm{diag}(\pmb{\lambda})$ , we may write the iterations (4.2-4.3) equivalently as

$$
\mathbf {r} _ {t} = \mathbf {o u} _ {t} \tag {a.1}
$$

$$
\mathbf {s} _ {t} = \mathbf {o} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\lambda} \mathbf {r} _ {t} \tag {a.2}
$$

$$
\mathbf {z} _ {t} = \mathbf {s} _ {t} - b _ {t 1} \mathbf {u} _ {1} - \dots - b _ {t t} \mathbf {u} _ {t} \tag {a.3}
$$

$$
\mathbf {u} _ {t + 1} = u _ {t + 1} \left(\mathbf {z} _ {1}, \dots , \mathbf {z} _ {t}, \mathbf {e}\right) \tag {a.4}
$$

as discussed in section 6, we will wish to identify the almost-sure limits

$$
\lim  _ {n \rightarrow \infty} n ^ {- 1} \mathbf {r} _ {s} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\lambda} ^ {k} \mathbf {r} _ {s ^ {\prime}} \equiv \lim  _ {n \rightarrow \infty} n ^ {- 1} \mathbf {u} _ {s} ^ {\top} \mathbf {w} ^ {k} \mathbf {u} _ {s ^ {\prime}} \tag {a.5}
$$

for each fixed pair  $s, s' \geq 1$  and fixed order  $k \geq 0$ . in section a.1 below, we first define certain "partial moment" coefficients  $c_{k,j}$  corresponding to the free cumulants  $\{\kappa_k\}_{k \geq 1}$  of  $\pmb{\lambda}$ . we then define, for each iteration  $t \geq 1$  and each order  $k \geq 0$ , the  $t \times t$  matrix

$$
\mathbf {l} _ {t} ^ {(k)} = \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {\infty} c _ {k, j} \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {t} ^ {(j)} \tag {a.6}
$$

where  $\theta_t^{(j)}$  is defined by (4.5). as in the definitions (4.7), this series is in fact finite because  $\theta_t^{(j)} = 0$  for  $j \geq 2t - 1$ . the limits (a.5) will be identified as the entries of  $\mathbf{l}_t^{(k,\infty)} = \lim_{n \to \infty} \mathbf{l}_t^{(k)}$ .

a.1. coefficients for "partial moments". let  $\{m_k\}_{k\geq 1}$  and  $\{\kappa_k\}_{k\geq 1}$  be the moments and free cumulants of  $\lambda$ , as defined in section 4.1. for notational convenience, we identify

$$
\kappa_ {0} = 1. \tag {a.7}
$$

we then define a doubly-indexed sequence of coefficients  $(c_{k,j})_{k,j\geq 0}$  by

$$
c _ {0, 0} = 1, \quad c _ {0, j} = 0 \text {f o r} j \geq 1, \quad c _ {k, j} = \sum_ {m = 0} ^ {j + 1} c _ {k - 1, m} \kappa_ {j + 1 - m} \text {f o r} k \geq 1. \tag {a.8}
$$

these coefficients admit the following combinatorial interpretation: let

$$
\operatorname {n c} (k, \ell) = \left\{\pi \in \operatorname {n c} (k): s \cap \{1, \dots , \ell \} \neq s \text {f o r a l l} s \in \pi \right\}.
$$

this is the subset of non-crossing partitions  $\pi \in \mathrm{nc}(k)$  where no set  $s \in \pi$  is contained in  $\{1, \dots, \ell\}$ . for  $\ell = 0$ ,  $\mathrm{nc}(k, 0) = \mathrm{nc}(k)$  is the set of all non-crossing partitions. the following lemma shows that  $c_{k,j}$  corresponds to the part of the sum (2.3) that enumerates only over the partitions belonging to the subset  $\mathrm{nc}(k + j, j)$  of  $\mathrm{nc}(k + j)$ .

lemma a.1. for each  $k \geq 1$ ,

$$
c _ {k, j} = \sum_ {\pi \in \mathrm {n c} (k + j, j)} \prod_ {s \in \pi} \kappa_ {| s |}. \tag {a.9}
$$

in particular,  $c_{1,j} = \kappa_{j+1}$  for each  $j \geq 0$ , and  $c_{k,0} = m_k$  for each  $k \geq 1$ .

proof. for  $k = 1$ , the only non-zero term in the sum (a.8) corresponds to  $m = 0$ . this gives  $c_{1,j} = \kappa_{j+1}$ . the only partition of  $\{1, \ldots, j+1\}$  where no set belongs to  $\{1, \ldots, j\}$  is the partition consisting of a single set with all  $j+1$  elements. thus  $\mathrm{nc}(j+1,j)$  consists of this single partition, so the right side of (a.9) is simply  $\kappa_{j+1}$ . this verifies (a.9) for  $k = 1$ .

suppose inductively that (a.9) holds for  $k - 1$  (and all  $j$ ). consider

$$
c _ {k, j} = c _ {k - 1, j + 1} + \sum_ {m = 0} ^ {j} c _ {k - 1, m} \kappa_ {j + 1 - m}. \tag {a.10}
$$

by this induction hypothesis, the first term is

$$
c _ {k - 1, j + 1} = \sum_ {\pi \in \mathrm {n c} (k + j, j + 1)} \prod_ {s \in \pi} \kappa_ {| s |}. \tag {a.11}
$$

to analyze the second term of (a.10), note that if  $\pi \in \mathrm{nc}(k + j,j)$  but  $\pi \notin \mathrm{nc}(k + j,j + 1)$ , then there is some set  $s \in \pi$  containing  $j + 1$  and also belonging to  $\{1,\dots ,j + 1\}$ . this set  $s \in \pi$  must consist of consecutive elements of  $\{1,\ldots ,j + 1\}$ , because if there is a gap in the elements of  $s$ , then the elements in this gap must form their own sets of  $\pi$  as  $\pi$  is non-crossing, and this contradicts  $\pi \in \mathrm{nc}(k + j,j)$ . thus  $s = \{m + 1,\dots ,j + 1\}$  for some  $m \in \{0,\dots ,j\}$ . removing  $s$  from  $\pi$  establishes a bijection between such partitions  $\pi$  and the non-crossing partitions  $\pi^{\prime} \in \mathrm{nc}(k - 1 + m,m)$  of the  $k - 1 + m$  remaining elements, such that no set of  $\pi^{\prime}$  is contained in  $\{1,\dots ,m\}$ . summing over such partitions  $\pi^{\prime}$  and applying the induction hypothesis, we have

$$
c_{k - 1,m} = \sum_{\pi^{\prime}\in \mathrm{nc}(k - 1 + m,m)}\prod_{s^{\prime}\in \pi^{\prime}}\kappa_{|s^{\prime}|}.
$$

then applying this bijection and including back  $\{m + 1,\dots ,j + 1\}$  (of size  $j + 1 - m$  ) into  $\pi$

$$
c_{k - 1,m}\kappa_{j + 1 - m} = \sum_{\substack{\pi \in \mathrm{nc}(k + j,j)\setminus \mathrm{nc}(k + j,j + 1)\\ \{m + 1,\ldots ,j + 1\} \in \pi}}\prod_{s\in \pi}\kappa_{|s|}.
$$

summing this over all possible values  $m \in \{0, \dots, j\}$  gives

$$
\sum_ {m = 0} ^ {j} c _ {k - 1, m} \kappa_ {j + 1 - m} = \sum_ {\pi \in \mathrm {n c} (k + j, j) \backslash \mathrm {n c} (k + j, j + 1)} \prod_ {s \in \pi} \kappa_ {| s |},
$$

and combining with (a.10) and (a.11) yields (a.9). this completes the induction, establishing (a.9) for all  $k$ .

finally, the statement  $c_{k,0} = m_k$  follows from specializing (a.9) to  $j = 0$ , and applying  $\mathrm{nc}(k,0) = \mathrm{nc}(k)$  and the moment-cumulant relations (2.3).

a.2. partial moment identities. recalling the definition of  $\mathbf{l}_t^{(k)}$  in (a.6), we now establish several identities that are derived from the recursion for  $c_{k,j}$  in (a.8).

lemma a.2. for every  $t \geq 1$ ,

$$
\mathbf {l} _ {t} ^ {(0)} = \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {t} \tag {a.12}
$$

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbf {l} _ {t} ^ {(1)} = \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {t} \mathbf {b} _ {t} + \boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {t} \\ = \mathbf {b} _ {t} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {t} + \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {t} \boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} ^ {\top} \tag {a.13} \\ \end{array}
$$

$$
\mathbf {l} _ {t} ^ {(2)} = \mathbf {b} _ {t} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {t} \mathbf {b} _ {t} + \mathbf {b} _ {t} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {t} + \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {t} \boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} ^ {\top} \mathbf {b} _ {t} + \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {t} \tag {a.14}
$$

proof. for  $k = 0$ , we have  $c_{0,0} = 1$  and  $c_{0,j} = 0$  for all  $j \geq 1$ . we also have  $\theta_t^{(0)} = \pmb{\delta}_t$ . hence (a.12) follows from (a.6).

for  $k = 1$ , we have  $c_{1,j} = \kappa_{j + 1}$  by lemma a.1. then

$$
\mathbf {l} _ {t} ^ {(1)} = \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {\infty} \kappa_ {j + 1} \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {t} ^ {(j)} = \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {\infty} \kappa_ {j + 1} \sum_ {i = 0} ^ {j} \boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} ^ {i} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {t} \big (\boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} ^ {j - i} \big) ^ {\top}.
$$

note that all series throughout this proof are actually finite, so we may freely exchange orders of summation. separating the terms that begin with  $\delta_t$  from those that begin with  $\phi_t$ ,

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbf {l} _ {t} ^ {(1)} = \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {\infty} \kappa_ {j + 1} \pmb {\delta} _ {t} (\pmb {\phi} _ {t} ^ {j}) ^ {\top} + \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {\infty} \kappa_ {j + 1} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {j} \pmb {\phi} _ {t} ^ {i} \pmb {\delta} _ {t} (\pmb {\phi} _ {t} ^ {j - i}) ^ {\top} \\ = \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {t} \mathbf {b} _ {t} + \boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {\infty} \kappa_ {j + 2} \sum_ {i = 0} ^ {j} \boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} ^ {i} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {t} (\boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} ^ {j - i}) ^ {\top} = \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {t} \mathbf {b} _ {t} + \boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {t}. \\ \end{array}
$$

since  $\mathbf{l}_t^{(1)}$ ,  $\pmb{\delta}_{t}$ , and  $\pmb{\sigma}_{t}$  are symmetric, we must also have  $\mathbf{l}_t^{(1)} = \mathbf{b}_t^\top \pmb{\delta}_t + \pmb{\sigma}_t\pmb{\phi}_t^\top$ , and this yields both identities in (a.13).

for  $k = 2$ , applying  $c_{1,m} = \kappa_{m + 1}$  and the recursion (a.8), we have

$$
\mathbf {l} _ {t} ^ {(2)} = \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {\infty} c _ {2, j} \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {t} ^ {(j)} = \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {\infty} \left(\sum_ {m = 0} ^ {j + 1} \kappa_ {m + 1} \kappa_ {j + 1 - m}\right) \cdot \left(\sum_ {i = 0} ^ {j} \boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} ^ {i} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {t} \big (\boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} ^ {j - i} \big) ^ {\top}\right).
$$

collecting terms by powers of  $\phi_t$  and  $\phi_t^\top$ ,

$$
\mathbf {l} _ {t} ^ {(2)} = \sum_ {i = 0} ^ {\infty} \sum_ {p = 0} ^ {\infty} \left(\sum_ {m = 0} ^ {i + p + 1} \kappa_ {m + 1} \kappa_ {i + p + 1 - m}\right) \boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} ^ {i} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {t} \big (\boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} ^ {p} \big) ^ {\top}.
$$

substituting  $q = i + p - m$ , we may write

$$
\sum_ {m = 0} ^ {i + p + 1} \kappa_ {m + 1} \kappa_ {i + p + 1 - m} = \kappa_ {i + 1} \kappa_ {p + 1} + \kappa_ {i + p + 2} \kappa_ {0} + \sum_ {m = 0} ^ {i - 1} \kappa_ {m + 1} \kappa_ {i + p + 1 - m} + \sum_ {q = 0} ^ {p - 1} \kappa_ {i + p + 1 - q} \kappa_ {q + 1}
$$

where the last two sums may be empty if  $i = 0$  or  $p = 0$ . recalling the notation  $\kappa_0 = 1$  from (a.7), and identifying

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbf {b} _ {t} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {t} \mathbf {b} _ {t} = \left(\sum_ {i = 0} ^ {\infty} \kappa_ {i + 1} \boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} ^ {i}\right) \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {t} \left(\sum_ {p = 0} ^ {\infty} \kappa_ {p + 1} (\boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} ^ {p}) ^ {\top}\right) = \sum_ {i = 0} ^ {\infty} \sum_ {p = 0} ^ {\infty} \kappa_ {i + 1} \kappa_ {p + 1} \boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} ^ {i} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {t} (\boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} ^ {p}) ^ {\top} \\ \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {t} = \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {\infty} \kappa_ {j + 2} \sum_ {i = 0} ^ {j} \phi_ {t} ^ {i} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {t} (\phi_ {t} ^ {j - i}) ^ {\top} = \sum_ {i = 0} ^ {\infty} \sum_ {p = 0} ^ {\infty} \kappa_ {i + p + 2} \kappa_ {0} \phi_ {t} ^ {i} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {t} (\phi_ {t} ^ {p}) ^ {\top} \\ \end{array}
$$

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbf {b} _ {t} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {t} = \left(\sum_ {m = 0} ^ {\infty} \kappa_ {m + 1} \boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} ^ {m}\right) \boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} \left(\sum_ {j = 0} ^ {\infty} \kappa_ {j + 2} \sum_ {p = 0} ^ {j} \boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} ^ {j - p} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {t} (\boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} ^ {p}) ^ {\top}\right) \\ = \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {\infty} \sum_ {p = 0} ^ {\infty} \left(\sum_ {m = 0} ^ {i - 1} \kappa_ {m + 1} \kappa_ {i + p + 1 - m}\right) \phi_ {t} ^ {i} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {t} \big (\phi_ {t} ^ {p} \big) ^ {\top} \\ \end{array}
$$

$$
\boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {t} \boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} ^ {\top} \mathbf {b} _ {t} = \left(\sum_ {j = 0} ^ {\infty} \kappa_ {j + 2} \sum_ {i = 0} ^ {j} \boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} ^ {i} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {t} (\boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} ^ {j - i}) ^ {\top}\right) \boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} ^ {\top} \left(\sum_ {q = 0} ^ {\infty} \kappa_ {q + 1} (\boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} ^ {q}) ^ {\top}\right)
$$

$$
= \sum_ {i = 0} ^ {\infty} \sum_ {p = 1} ^ {\infty} \left(\sum_ {q = 0} ^ {p - 1} \kappa_ {i + p + 1 - q} \kappa_ {q + 1}\right) \boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} ^ {i} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {t} \left(\boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} ^ {p}\right) ^ {\top},
$$

this yields (a.14).

lemma a.3. define

$$
\boldsymbol {\upsilon} _ {t} = \left( \begin{array}{c c} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {t} & \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {t} \mathbf {b} _ {t} + \boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {t} \\ \boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} ^ {\top} & \boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} ^ {\top} \mathbf {b} _ {t} + \operatorname {i d} \end{array} \right). \tag {a.15}
$$

for every  $t \geq 1$  and  $k \geq 0$ ,

$$
\left(\mathbf {l} _ {t} ^ {(k)} \quad \mathbf {l} _ {t} ^ {(k + 1)}\right) = \left(\sum_ {j = 0} ^ {\infty} c _ {k, j} \boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} ^ {j} \quad \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {\infty} c _ {k, j + 1} \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {t} ^ {(j)}\right) \boldsymbol {\upsilon} _ {t} \tag {a.16}
$$

$$
\left( \begin{array}{l l} \mathbf {l} _ {t} ^ {(k)} & \mathbf {l} _ {t} ^ {(k + 1)} \\ \mathbf {l} _ {t} ^ {(k + 1)} & \mathbf {l} _ {t} ^ {(k + 2)} \end{array} \right) = c _ {k, 0} \left( \begin{array}{l l} \mathbf {l} _ {t} ^ {(0)} & \mathbf {l} _ {t} ^ {(1)} \\ \mathbf {l} _ {t} ^ {(1)} & \mathbf {l} _ {t} ^ {(2)} \end{array} \right) + \boldsymbol {\upsilon} _ {t} ^ {\top} \left( \begin{array}{c c} 0 & \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {\infty} c _ {k, j + 1} \left(\boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} ^ {j}\right) ^ {\top} \\ \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {\infty} c _ {k, j + 1} \boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} ^ {j} & \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {\infty} c _ {k, j + 2} \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {t} ^ {(j)} \end{array} \right) \boldsymbol {\upsilon} _ {t} \tag {a.17}
$$

proof. applying (a.13) and the definitions of  $\mathbf{l}_t^{(1)}$  and  $\mathbf{b}_t$ , and recalling the notation  $\kappa_0 = 1$  from (a.7) and  $c_{1,j} = \kappa_{j + 1}$  from lemma a.1,

$$
\boldsymbol {\upsilon} _ {t} ^ {\top} = \left( \begin{array}{c c} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {t} & \boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} \\ \mathbf {l} _ {t} ^ {(1)} & \mathbf {b} _ {t} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} + \operatorname {i d} \end{array} \right) = \left( \begin{array}{c c} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {t} & \boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} \\ \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {\infty} \kappa_ {j + 1} \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {t} ^ {(j)} & \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {\infty} \kappa_ {j} \boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} ^ {j} \end{array} \right). \tag {a.18}
$$

for (a.16), applying the definition of  $\theta_t^{(j)}$ , we compute

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left( \begin{array}{c c} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {t} & \boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} \end{array} \right) \left( \begin{array}{c} \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {\infty} c _ {k, j} (\boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} ^ {j}) ^ {\top} \\ \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {\infty} c _ {k, j + 1} \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {t} ^ {(j)} \end{array} \right) = \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {t} \cdot \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {\infty} c _ {k, j} (\boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} ^ {j}) ^ {\top} + \boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} \cdot \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {\infty} c _ {k, j + 1} \sum_ {i = 0} ^ {j} \boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} ^ {i} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {t} (\boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} ^ {j - i}) ^ {\top} \\ = \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {\infty} c _ {k, j} \sum_ {i = 0} ^ {j} \phi_ {t} ^ {i} \pmb {\delta} _ {t} \big (\phi_ {t} ^ {j - i} \big) ^ {\top} \\ = \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {\infty} c _ {k, j} \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {t} ^ {(j)} = \mathbf {l} _ {t} ^ {(k)}. \tag {a.19} \\ \end{array}
$$

we also compute

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left( \begin{array}{c c} \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {\infty} \kappa_ {j + 1} \pmb {\theta} _ {t} ^ {(j)} & \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {\infty} \kappa_ {j} \pmb {\phi} _ {t} ^ {j} \end{array} \right) \left( \begin{array}{c} \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {\infty} c _ {k, j} (\pmb {\phi} _ {t} ^ {j}) ^ {\top} \\ \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {\infty} c _ {k, j + 1} \pmb {\theta} _ {t} ^ {(j)} \end{array} \right) \\ = \left(\sum_ {j = 0} ^ {\infty} \kappa_ {j + 1} \sum_ {i = 0} ^ {j} \phi_ {t} ^ {i} \delta_ {t} \left(\phi_ {t} ^ {j - i}\right) ^ {\top}\right) \cdot \left(\sum_ {p = 0} ^ {\infty} c _ {k, p} \left(\phi_ {t} ^ {p}\right) ^ {\top}\right) \\ + \left(\sum_ {j = 0} ^ {\infty} \kappa_ {j} \phi_ {t} ^ {j}\right) \cdot \left(\sum_ {p = 0} ^ {\infty} c _ {k, p + 1} \sum_ {q = 0} ^ {p} \phi_ {t} ^ {p - q} \delta_ {t} \big (\phi_ {t} ^ {q} \big) ^ {\top}\right) \\ = \sum_ {i = 0} ^ {\infty} \sum_ {j = i} ^ {\infty} \sum_ {p = 0} ^ {\infty} \kappa_ {j + 1} c _ {k, p} \phi_ {t} ^ {i} \pmb {\delta} _ {t} (\pmb {\phi} _ {t} ^ {j - i + p}) ^ {\top} + \sum_ {q = 0} ^ {\infty} \sum_ {p = q} ^ {\infty} \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {\infty} \kappa_ {j} c _ {k, p + 1} \phi_ {t} ^ {j + p - q} \pmb {\delta} _ {t} (\pmb {\phi} _ {t} ^ {q}) ^ {\top} \\ = \sum_ {i = 0} ^ {\infty} \sum_ {r = 0} ^ {\infty} \left(\sum_ {j = i} ^ {r + i} \kappa_ {j + 1} c _ {k, r - j + i}\right) \phi_ {t} ^ {i} \pmb {\delta} _ {t} \big (\phi_ {t} ^ {r} \big) ^ {\top} + \sum_ {q = 0} ^ {\infty} \sum_ {\ell = 0} ^ {\infty} \left(\sum_ {p = q} ^ {\ell + q} \kappa_ {\ell - p + q} c _ {k, p + 1}\right) \phi_ {t} ^ {\ell} \pmb {\delta} _ {t} \big (\phi_ {t} ^ {q} \big) ^ {\top} \\ = \sum_ {i = 0} ^ {\infty} \sum_ {r = 0} ^ {\infty} \left(\left(\kappa_ {i + 1} c _ {k, r} + \kappa_ {i + 2} c _ {k, r - 1} + + \dots + \kappa_ {i + r + 1} c _ {k, 0}\right) \right. \\ \end{array}
$$

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left. + \left(\kappa_ {i} c _ {k, r + 1} + \kappa_ {i - 1} c _ {k, r + 2} + \ldots + \kappa_ {0} c _ {k, i + r + 1}\right)\right) \phi_ {t} ^ {i} \delta_ {t} \big (\phi_ {t} ^ {r} \big) ^ {\top} \\ = \sum_ {i = 0} ^ {\infty} \sum_ {r = 0} ^ {\infty} \left(\sum_ {j = 0} ^ {i + r + 1} \kappa_ {j} c _ {k, i + r + 1 - j}\right) \boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} ^ {i} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {t} \left(\boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} ^ {r}\right) ^ {\top}. \tag {a.20} \\ \end{array}
$$

from the recursion for  $c_{k,j}$  in (a.8), this is equal to

$$
\sum_ {i = 0} ^ {\infty} \sum_ {r = 0} ^ {\infty} c _ {k + 1, i + r} \phi_ {t} ^ {i} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {t} \left(\phi_ {t} ^ {r}\right) ^ {\top} = \mathbf {l} _ {t} ^ {(k + 1)}.
$$

combining this with (a.19) and (a.18) and taking the transpose yields (a.16).

for (a.17), applying (a.18), first observe that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbf {\boldsymbol {\upsilon}} _ {t} ^ {\top} \left( \begin{array}{c} 0 \\ \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {\infty} c _ {k, j + 1} \mathbf {\phi} _ {t} ^ {j} \end{array} \right) = \left( \begin{array}{c} \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {\infty} c _ {k, j + 1} \mathbf {\phi} _ {t} ^ {j + 1} \\ \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {\infty} \kappa_ {j} \mathbf {\phi} _ {t} ^ {j} \cdot \sum_ {p = 0} ^ {\infty} c _ {k, p + 1} \mathbf {\phi} _ {t} ^ {p} \end{array} \right) \\ = \left( \begin{array}{c} \sum_ {\ell = 1} ^ {\infty} c _ {k, \ell} \boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} ^ {\ell} \\ \sum_ {\ell = 0} ^ {\infty} \left(\sum_ {j = 0} ^ {\ell} \kappa_ {j} c _ {k, \ell - j + 1}\right) \boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} ^ {\ell} \end{array} \right) = \left( \begin{array}{c} \sum_ {\ell = 0} ^ {\infty} (c _ {k, \ell} - c _ {k, 0} c _ {0, \ell}) \boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} ^ {\ell} \\ \sum_ {\ell = 0} ^ {\infty} (c _ {k + 1, \ell} - c _ {k, 0} c _ {1, \ell}) \boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} ^ {\ell} \end{array} \right) \\ \end{array}
$$

where the last equality applies  $c_{0,0} = 1$ ,  $c_{0,\ell} = 0$  for  $\ell \geq 1$ ,  $c_{1,\ell} = \kappa_{\ell + 1}$ , and the recursion (a.8). next, applying the same computations as leading to (a.19) and (a.20), we obtain

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbf {\boldsymbol {\tau}} _ {t} ^ {\top} \left( \begin{array}{c} \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {\infty} c _ {k, j + 1} (\mathbf {\boldsymbol {\phi}} _ {t} ^ {j}) ^ {\top} \\ \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {\infty} c _ {k, j + 2} \mathbf {\boldsymbol {\theta}} _ {t} ^ {(j)} \end{array} \right) = \left( \begin{array}{c} \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {\infty} c _ {k, j + 1} \mathbf {\boldsymbol {\theta}} _ {t} ^ {(j)} \\ \sum_ {i = 0} ^ {\infty} \sum_ {r = 0} ^ {\infty} \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {i + r + 1} \kappa_ {j} c _ {k, i + r + 2 - j} \mathbf {\boldsymbol {\phi}} _ {t} ^ {i} \mathbf {\boldsymbol {\delta}} _ {t} (\mathbf {\boldsymbol {\phi}} _ {t} ^ {r}) ^ {\top} \end{array} \right) \\ = \left( \begin{array}{c} \sum_ {\ell = 0} ^ {\infty} (c _ {k, \ell + 1} - c _ {k, 0} c _ {0, \ell + 1}) \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {t} ^ {(\ell)} \\ \sum_ {\ell = 0} ^ {\infty} (c _ {k + 1, \ell + 1} - c _ {k, 0} c _ {1, \ell + 1}) \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {t} ^ {(\ell)} \end{array} \right) \\ \end{array}
$$

where the second equality again applies  $c_{0,\ell +1} = 0$  for  $\ell \geq 0$ ,  $c_{1,\ell +1} = \kappa_{\ell +2}$ , and the recursion (a.8). combining these two identities, we get

$$
\begin{array}{l} \boldsymbol {\upsilon} _ {t} ^ {\top} \left( \begin{array}{c c} 0 & \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {\infty} c _ {k, j + 1} (\boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} ^ {j}) ^ {\top} \\ \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {\infty} c _ {k, j + 1} \boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} ^ {j} & \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {\infty} c _ {k, j + 2} \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {t} ^ {(j)} \end{array} \right) \\ = \left( \begin{array}{c c} \sum_ {\ell = 0} ^ {\infty} c _ {k, \ell} \phi_ {t} ^ {\ell} & \sum_ {\ell = 0} ^ {\infty} c _ {k, \ell + 1} \theta_ {t} ^ {(\ell)} \\ \sum_ {\ell = 0} ^ {\infty} c _ {k + 1, \ell} \phi_ {t} ^ {\ell} & \sum_ {\ell = 0} ^ {\infty} c _ {k + 1, \ell + 1} \theta_ {t} ^ {(\ell)} \end{array} \right) - c _ {k, 0} \left( \begin{array}{c c} \sum_ {\ell = 0} ^ {\infty} c _ {0, \ell} \phi_ {t} ^ {\ell} & \sum_ {\ell = 0} ^ {\infty} c _ {0, \ell + 1} \theta_ {t} ^ {(\ell)} \\ \sum_ {\ell = 0} ^ {\infty} c _ {1, \ell} \phi_ {t} ^ {\ell} & \sum_ {\ell = 0} ^ {\infty} c _ {1, \ell + 1} \theta_ {t} ^ {(\ell)} \end{array} \right) \\ \end{array}
$$

then (a.17) follows from multiplying on the right by  $\mathbf{\upsilon}_t$ , and applying (a.16) to the right side with  $k$  and also with  $0, 1, k + 1$  in place of  $k$ .

a.3. conditioning argument. we now prove theorem 4.3, applying the conditioning argument described in section 6. theorem 4.3 follows directly from the following extended lemma, where part (b) identifies the limits (a.5) with the limit of  $\mathbf{l}_t^{(k)}$ .

lemma a.4. suppose assumption 4.2 holds. almost surely for each  $t = 1,2,3,\ldots$

(a) there exist deterministic matrices  $(\pmb{\delta}_t^\infty, \pmb{\phi}_t^\infty, \pmb{\theta}_t^{(j,\infty)}, \mathbf{b}_t^\infty, \pmb{\sigma}_t^\infty, \mathbf{l}_t^{(k,\infty)})$  for all fixed  $j, k \geq 0$  such that

$$
(\pmb {\delta} _ {t} ^ {\infty}, \pmb {\phi} _ {t} ^ {\infty}, \pmb {\theta} _ {t} ^ {(j, \infty)}, \mathbf {b} _ {t} ^ {\infty}, \pmb {\sigma} _ {t} ^ {\infty}, \mathbf {l} _ {t} ^ {(k, \infty)}) = \lim _ {n \to \infty} (\pmb {\delta} _ {t}, \pmb {\phi} _ {t}, \pmb {\theta} _ {t} ^ {(j)}, \mathbf {b} _ {t}, \pmb {\sigma} _ {t}, \mathbf {l} _ {t} ^ {(k)}).
$$

(b) for some random variables  $r_{1},\ldots ,r_{t}$  having finite moments of all orders,

$$
\left(\mathbf {r} _ {1}, \ldots , \mathbf {r} _ {t}, \boldsymbol {\lambda}\right) \stackrel {w} {\rightarrow} \left(r _ {1}, \ldots , r _ {t}, \boldsymbol {\lambda}\right).
$$

furthermore, for each  $k \geq 0$ ,

$$
\mathbb {e} [ (r _ {1}, \ldots , r _ {t}) ^ {\top} \lambda^ {k} (r _ {1}, \ldots , r _ {t}) ] \equiv \lim _ {n \to \infty} n ^ {- 1} (\mathbf {r} _ {1}, \ldots , \mathbf {r} _ {t}) ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\lambda} ^ {k} (\mathbf {r} _ {1}, \ldots , \mathbf {r} _ {t}) = \mathbf {l} _ {t} ^ {(k, \infty)}.
$$

(c) we have

$$
\left(\mathbf {u} _ {1}, \dots , \mathbf {u} _ {t + 1}, \mathbf {z} _ {1}, \dots , \mathbf {z} _ {t}, \mathbf {e}\right) \stackrel {{w}} {{\rightarrow}} \left(u _ {1}, \dots , u _ {t + 1}, z _ {1}, \dots , z _ {t}, e\right)
$$

as described in theorem 4.3.

(d) the matrix

$$
\left( \begin{array}{c c} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {t} ^ {\infty} & \boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} ^ {\infty} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {t} ^ {\infty} \\ \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {t} ^ {\infty} (\boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} ^ {\infty}) ^ {\top} & \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {t} ^ {\infty} \end{array} \right)
$$

is non-singular.

proof. denote by  $t^{(a)}, t^{(b)}, t^{(c)}, t^{(d)}$  the claims of parts (a-d) up to and including iteration  $t$ . we induct on  $t$ . note that since  $\pmb{\lambda} \xrightarrow{w} \pmb{\lambda}$  by assumption 4.2(b), the empirical moments  $m_{k}$  of  $\pmb{\lambda}$  satisfy  $m_{k} \to m_{k}^{\infty} \equiv \mathbb{e}[\lambda^{k}]$  for each  $k \geq 0$ . then also

$$
\kappa_ {k} \rightarrow \kappa_ {k} ^ {\infty}, \qquad c _ {k, j} \rightarrow c _ {k, j} ^ {\infty}
$$

for all  $j, k \geq 0$ , where  $\kappa_{k}^{\infty}$  and  $c_{k,j}^{\infty}$  are the free cumulants and partial moment coefficients of  $\lambda$ .

step 1:  $t = 1$ . we have  $\pmb{\delta}_{1} = \langle \mathbf{u}_{1}^{2}\rangle \rightarrow \pmb{\delta}_{1}^{\infty} \equiv \mathbb{e}[u_{1}^{2}]$  by assumption 4.2(c),  $\kappa_{k} \rightarrow \kappa_{k}^{\infty}$  and  $c_{k,j} \rightarrow c_{k,j}^{\infty}$  by the above, and  $\phi_{1} = 0$ . then  $1^{(a)}$  follows from the definitions.

noting that  $\mathbf{r}_1 = \mathbf{o}\mathbf{u}_1$  and applying proposition f.2 with  $\pi = \mathrm{id}$ , we have

$$
\left(\boldsymbol {\lambda}, \mathbf {r} _ {1}\right) \stackrel {{w}} {{\rightarrow}} \left(\lambda , r _ {1}\right)
$$

where  $r_{1}\sim \mathcal{n}(0,\mathbb{e}[u_{1}^{2}])$  is independent of  $\lambda$ . then for any  $k\geq 0$

$$
n ^ {- 1} \mathbf {r} _ {1} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\lambda} ^ {k} \mathbf {r} _ {1} = n ^ {- 1} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \lambda_ {i} ^ {k} r _ {i 1} ^ {2} \to \mathbb {e} [ \boldsymbol {\lambda} ^ {k} r _ {1} ^ {2} ] = m _ {k} ^ {\infty} \mathbb {e} [ u _ {1} ^ {2} ].
$$

note that  $m_{k}^{\infty} = c_{k,0}^{\infty}$  by lemma a.1. furthermore,  $\phi_1 = 0$  so that  $\theta_1^{(0)} = \pmb{\delta}_1 = \langle \mathbf{u}_1^2\rangle$  and  $\theta_1^{(j)} = 0$  for all  $j\geq 1$ . hence  $\mathbf{l}_1^{(k,\infty)} = m_k^\infty \mathbb{e}[u_1^2 ]$  for each  $k\geq 0$ . this shows  $1^{(b)}$ .

for  $1^{(c)}$ , conditioning on  $\mathbf{u}_1, \mathbf{r}_1, \boldsymbol{\lambda}, \mathbf{e}$ , the conditional law of  $\mathbf{o}$  is that of  $\mathbf{o}$  conditioned on the event

$$
\mathbf {r} _ {1} = \mathbf {o u} _ {1}.
$$

since  $n^{-1}\| \mathbf{r}_1\|^2 \to \mathbb{e}[r_1^2] = \mathbb{e}[u_1^2]$ , and this is non-zero by assumption 4.2(e), we must have  $\mathbf{r}_1 \neq 0$  for all large  $n$ . then by proposition f.1, this conditional law of  $\mathbf{o}$  is equal to

$$
\mathbf {r} _ {1} (\mathbf {r} _ {1} ^ {\top} \mathbf {r} _ {1}) ^ {- 1} \mathbf {u} _ {1} ^ {\top} + \pi_ {\mathbf {r} _ {1} ^ {\bot}} \tilde {\mathbf {o}} \pi_ {\mathbf {u} _ {1} ^ {\bot}} ^ {\top}
$$

where  $\tilde{\mathbf{o}}\in \mathbb{r}^{(n - 1)\times (n - 1)}$  is haar-uniform and independent of  $(\mathbf{u}_1,\mathbf{r}_1,\pmb {\lambda},\mathbf{e})$ , and  $\pi_{\mathbf{r}_1^\perp},\pi_{\mathbf{u}_1^\perp}\in \mathbb{r}^{n\times (n - 1)}$  have orthonormal columns spanning the orthogonal complements of  $\mathbf{r}_1,\mathbf{u}_1$ . thus, to analyze the joint behavior of  $(\mathbf{u}_1,\mathbf{u}_2,\mathbf{z}_1,\mathbf{e})$ , we may replace the update  $\mathbf{s}_1 = \mathbf{o}^\top \pmb {\lambda}\mathbf{r}_1$  in this first iteration  $t = 1$  by the update

$$
\mathbf {s} _ {1} = \mathbf {s} _ {\parallel} + \mathbf {s} _ {\perp}
$$

$$
\mathbf {s} _ {\parallel} = \mathbf {u} _ {1} \left(\mathbf {r} _ {1} ^ {\top} \mathbf {r} _ {1}\right) ^ {- 1} \mathbf {r} _ {1} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\lambda} \mathbf {r} _ {1}
$$

$$
\mathbf {s} _ {\perp} = \pi_ {\mathbf {u} _ {1} ^ {\perp}} \tilde {\mathbf {o}} ^ {\top} \pi_ {\mathbf {r} _ {1} ^ {\perp}} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\lambda} \mathbf {r} _ {1}
$$

as this will not change the joint law of  $(\mathbf{u}_1,\mathbf{u}_2,\mathbf{z}_1,\mathbf{e})$

for  $\mathbf{s}_{\parallel}$ , applying  $1^{(b)}$ , we have  $n^{-1}\mathbf{r}_1^\top \pmb{\lambda}\mathbf{r}_1 / (n^{-1}\mathbf{r}_1^\top \mathbf{r}_1) \to m_1^\infty \mathbb{e}[r_1^2] / \mathbb{e}[r_1^2] = \kappa_1^\infty$ . then applying  $(\mathbf{u}_1, \mathbf{e}) \xrightarrow{w} (u_1, e)$  and proposition e.4,

$$
\left(\mathbf {u} _ {1}, \mathbf {e}, \mathbf {s} _ {\parallel}\right) \xrightarrow {w} \left(u _ {1}, e, s _ {\parallel}\right), \qquad s _ {\parallel} = \kappa_ {1} ^ {\infty} u _ {1}.
$$

for  $\mathbf{s}_{\perp}$ , applying  $1^{(b)}$  again and identifying  $\kappa_2^\infty = m_2^\infty - (m_1^\infty)^2$ , observe that

$$
n ^ {- 1} \| \pi_ {\mathbf {r} _ {1} ^ {\perp}} ^ {\top} \mathbf {\lambda} \mathbf {r} _ {1} \| ^ {2} = n ^ {- 1} \| \mathbf {\lambda} \mathbf {r} _ {1} \| ^ {2} - \frac {(n ^ {- 1} \mathbf {r} _ {1} ^ {\top} \mathbf {\lambda} \mathbf {r} _ {1}) ^ {2}}{n ^ {- 1} \| \mathbf {r} _ {1} \| ^ {2}} \to m _ {2} ^ {\infty} \mathbb {e} [ u _ {1} ^ {2} ] - \frac {(m _ {1} ^ {\infty} \mathbb {e} [ u _ {1} ^ {2} ]) ^ {2}}{\mathbb {e} [ u _ {1} ^ {2} ]} = \kappa_ {2} ^ {\infty} \mathbb {e} [ u _ {1} ^ {2} ].
$$

then applying proposition f.2,

$$
\mathbf {s} _ {\perp} \stackrel {w} {\rightarrow} s _ {\perp} \sim \mathcal {n} (0, \kappa_ {2} ^ {\infty} \mathbb {e} [ u _ {1} ^ {2} ]),
$$

where this limit  $s_{\perp}$  is independent of  $(u_1,e)$ . observe that  $\mathbf{b}_1 = \kappa_1$ , so

$$
\mathbf {z} _ {1} = \mathbf {s} _ {1} - \kappa_ {1} \mathbf {u} _ {1} = \left(\mathbf {s} _ {\parallel} - \kappa_ {1} \mathbf {u} _ {1}\right) + \mathbf {s} _ {\perp}.
$$

applying  $\kappa_{1} \to \kappa_{1}^{\infty}$ ,  $s_{\parallel} = \kappa_{1}^{\infty} u_{1}$ , and propositions e.2 and e.4, we obtain

$$
\left(\mathbf {z} _ {1}, \mathbf {u} _ {1}, \mathbf {e}\right) \stackrel {{w}} {{\to}} \left(z _ {1}, u _ {1}, e\right), \qquad z _ {1} = s _ {\perp}.
$$

then also  $(\mathbf{z}_1,\mathbf{u}_1,\mathbf{u}_2,\mathbf{e})\stackrel {w}{\to} (z_1,u_1,u_2,e)$  where  $u_{2} = u_{2}(z_{1},e)$ , by proposition e.2 and the polynomial growth condition for  $u_{2}(\cdot)$  in assumption 4.2(d). identifying  $\pmb{\sigma}_{1}^{\infty} = \kappa_{2}^{\infty}\mathbb{e}[u_{1}^{2}]$  as the variance of  $s_{\perp}$ , this shows  $1^{(c)}$ .

finally, we have  $\phi_1^\infty = 0$ , and  $\kappa_2^\infty = \mathrm{var}[\lambda] > 0$  and  $\mathbb{e}[u_1^2] > 0$  by assumption 4.2(e). this implies  $1^{(d)}$ .

step 2: analysis of  $\mathbf{r}_{t + 1}$ . suppose that  $t^{(a)}, t^{(b)}, t^{(c)}, t^{(d)}$  all hold, and consider iteration  $t + 1$ . note that  $t^{(c)}$  implies  $\langle \mathbf{u}_s \mathbf{u}_{s'} \rangle \to \mathbb{e}[u_s u_{s'}]$  for all  $s, s' \leq t + 1$ , so  $\pmb{\delta}_{t + 1} \to \pmb{\delta}_{t + 1}^{\infty}$ . by assumption 4.2(d), for all  $s' < s \leq t + 1$ , each derivative  $\partial_{s'} u_s$  satisfies the growth condition (2.1) and is also continuous on a set of probability 1 under  $(z_1, \ldots, z_{s - 1}, e)$ , since  $\pmb{\sigma}_{s - 1}^{\infty}$  is non-singular by  $t^{(d)}$ . then by  $t^{(c)}$  and proposition e.3, we also have  $\langle \partial_{s'} \mathbf{u}_s \rangle \to \mathbb{e}[\partial_{s'} u_s(z_1, \ldots, z_{s - 1}, e)]$ , so  $\phi_{t + 1} \to \phi_{t + 1}^{\infty}$ . combining with the convergence  $\kappa_k \to \kappa_k^\infty$  and  $c_{k,j} \to c_{k,j}^\infty$  and the definitions, this yields  $t + 1^{(a)}$ .

let us now show  $t + 1^{(b)}$  by analyzing the iterate  $\mathbf{r}_{t+1}$ . we define the  $n \times t$  matrices

$$
\mathbf {u} _ {t} = \left(\mathbf {u} _ {1}, \ldots , \mathbf {u} _ {t}\right), \qquad \mathbf {r} _ {t} = \left(\mathbf {r} _ {1}, \ldots , \mathbf {r} _ {t}\right), \qquad \mathbf {z} _ {t} = \left(\mathbf {z} _ {1}, \ldots , \mathbf {z} _ {t}\right).
$$

then the updates (a.1-a.3) up to iteration  $t$  may be written as

$$
\mathbf {r} _ {t} = \mathbf {o} \mathbf {u} _ {t}, \qquad \mathbf {z} _ {t} = \mathbf {o} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\lambda} \mathbf {r} _ {t} - \mathbf {u} _ {t} \mathbf {b} _ {t},
$$

or equivalently,

$$
\mathbf {r} _ {t} = \mathbf {o u} _ {t}, \quad \mathbf {o z} _ {t} = \boldsymbol {\lambda} \mathbf {r} _ {t} - \mathbf {r} _ {t} \mathbf {b} _ {t}.
$$

thus, conditioning on  $\mathbf{u}_t,\mathbf{r}_t,\mathbf{z}_t,\mathbf{u}_{t + 1},\pmb {\lambda},\mathbf{e}$  , the law of  $\mathbf{o}$  is conditioned on the event

$$
\left( \begin{array}{c c} \mathbf {r} _ {t} & \boldsymbol {\lambda} \mathbf {r} _ {t} \end{array} \right) \left( \begin{array}{c c} \operatorname {i d} & - \mathbf {b} _ {t} \\ 0 & \operatorname {i d} \end{array} \right) = \mathbf {o} \left( \begin{array}{c c} \mathbf {u} _ {t} & \mathbf {z} _ {t} \end{array} \right).
$$

let us introduce

$$
\mathbf {m} _ {t} = n ^ {- 1} \left( \begin{array}{c c} \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\top} \mathbf {u} _ {t} & \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\top} \mathbf {z} _ {t} \\ \mathbf {z} _ {t} ^ {\top} \mathbf {u} _ {t} & \mathbf {z} _ {t} ^ {\top} \mathbf {z} _ {t} \end{array} \right).
$$

by  $t^{(c)}$ , we have  $n^{-1}\mathbf{u}_t^\top \mathbf{u}_t \to \pmb{\delta}_t^\infty$  and  $n^{-1}\mathbf{z}_t^\top \mathbf{z}_t \to \pmb{\sigma}_t^\infty$ . applying proposition e.5 (derived from stein's lemma) entrywise to  $n^{-1}\mathbf{u}_t^\top \mathbf{z}_t$ , and recalling the definition of  $\phi_t$  in (4.4), we also have  $n^{-1}\mathbf{u}_t^\top \mathbf{z}_t \to \phi_t^\infty \pmb{\sigma}_t^\infty$ . so

$$
\mathbf {m} _ {t} \rightarrow \mathbf {m} _ {t} ^ {\infty} = \left(\begin{array}{c c}\boldsymbol {\delta} _ {t} ^ {\infty}&\boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} ^ {\infty} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {t} ^ {\infty}\\\boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {t} ^ {\infty} (\boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} ^ {\infty}) ^ {\top}&\boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {t} ^ {\infty}\end{array}\right). \tag {a.21}
$$

this limit  $\mathbf{m}_t^\infty$  is invertible by  $t^{(d)}$ . then  $\mathbf{m}_t$  must have full rank  $2t$  for all large  $n$ , so  $(\mathbf{u}_t, \mathbf{z}_t)$  also has full column rank  $2t$  for all large  $n$ . then by proposition f.1, the above conditional law of  $\mathbf{o}$  is given by

$$
\left( \begin{array}{c c} \mathbf {r} _ {t} & \boldsymbol {\lambda} \mathbf {r} _ {t} \end{array} \right) \left( \begin{array}{c c} \mathrm {i d} & - \mathbf {b} _ {t} \\ 0 & \mathrm {i d} \end{array} \right) \mathbf {m} _ {t} ^ {- 1} \cdot n ^ {- 1} \left( \begin{array}{c} \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\top} \\ \mathbf {z} _ {t} ^ {\top} \end{array} \right) + \pi_ {(\mathbf {r} _ {t}, \boldsymbol {\lambda} \mathbf {r} _ {t}) ^ {\perp}} \tilde {\mathbf {o}} \pi_ {(\mathbf {u} _ {t}, \mathbf {z} _ {t}) ^ {\perp}} ^ {\top}
$$

where  $\tilde{\mathbf{o}}\in \mathbb{r}^{(n - 2t)\times (n - 2t)}$  is again an independent haar-orthogonal matrix. to analyze  $(\mathbf{r}_1,\dots ,\mathbf{r}_{t + 1},\pmb {\lambda})$  we may then replace the update  $\mathbf{r}_{t + 1} = \mathbf{o}\mathbf{u}_{t + 1}$  by

$$
\mathbf {r} _ {t + 1} = \mathbf {r} _ {\parallel} + \mathbf {r} _ {\perp}
$$

$$
\mathbf {r} _ {\parallel} = \left( \begin{array}{c c} \mathbf {r} _ {t} & \boldsymbol {\lambda} \mathbf {r} _ {t} \end{array} \right) \left( \begin{array}{c c} \operatorname {i d} & - \mathbf {b} _ {t} \\ 0 & \operatorname {i d} \end{array} \right) \mathbf {m} _ {t} ^ {- 1} \cdot n ^ {- 1} \left( \begin{array}{c} \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\top} \\ \mathbf {z} _ {t} ^ {\top} \end{array} \right) \mathbf {u} _ {t + 1}
$$

$$
\mathbf {r} _ {\perp} = \pi_ {(\mathbf {r} _ {t}, \boldsymbol {\lambda} \mathbf {r} _ {t}) ^ {\perp}} \tilde {\mathbf {o}} \pi_ {(\mathbf {u} _ {t}, \mathbf {z} _ {t}) ^ {\perp}} ^ {\top} \mathbf {u} _ {t + 1},
$$

as this does not change the joint law of  $(\mathbf{r}_1,\dots ,\mathbf{r}_{t + 1},\pmb {\lambda})$

to analyze  $\mathbf{r}_{\parallel}$ , let us define

$$
\boldsymbol {\delta} _ {t} ^ {\infty} = \left( \begin{array}{c} \mathbb {e} [ u _ {1} u _ {t + 1} ] \\ \vdots \\ \mathbb {e} [ u _ {t} u _ {t + 1} ] \end{array} \right), \qquad \boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} ^ {\infty} = \left( \begin{array}{c} \mathbb {e} [ \partial_ {1} u _ {t + 1} (z _ {1}, \ldots , z _ {t}, e) ] \\ \vdots \\ \mathbb {e} [ \partial_ {t} u _ {t + 1} (z _ {1}, \ldots , z _ {t}, e) ] \end{array} \right).
$$

these are the last columns of  $\pmb{\delta}_{t + 1}^{\infty}$  and  $(\phi_{t + 1}^{\infty})^{\top}$  with their last entries removed. then, applying again  $t^{(c)}$  and proposition e.5,

$$
n ^ {- 1} \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\top} \mathbf {u} _ {t + 1} \to \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {t} ^ {\infty}, \qquad n ^ {- 1} \mathbf {z} _ {t} ^ {\top} \mathbf {u} _ {t + 1} \to \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {t} ^ {\infty} \boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} ^ {\infty}.
$$

noting that  $\sigma_t^\infty$  is invertible by  $t^{(d)}$ , this yields

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left( \begin{array}{c c} \operatorname {i d} & - \mathbf {b} _ {t} \\ 0 & \operatorname {i d} \end{array} \right) \mathbf {m} _ {t} ^ {- 1} \cdot n ^ {- 1} \left( \begin{array}{c} \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\top} \\ \mathbf {z} _ {t} ^ {\top} \end{array} \right) \mathbf {u} _ {t + 1} \\ \rightarrow \left(\left(\begin{array}{c c}\mathrm {i d}&0\\0&(\boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {t} ^ {\infty}) ^ {- 1}\end{array}\right) \mathbf {m} _ {t} ^ {\infty} \left(\begin{array}{c c}\mathrm {i d}&\mathbf {b} _ {t} ^ {\infty}\\0&\mathrm {i d}\end{array}\right)\right) ^ {- 1} \left(\begin{array}{c}\boldsymbol {\delta} _ {t} ^ {\infty}\\\boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} ^ {\infty}\end{array}\right) = (\boldsymbol {\upsilon} _ {t} ^ {\infty}) ^ {- 1} \left(\begin{array}{c}\boldsymbol {\delta} _ {t} ^ {\infty}\\\boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} ^ {\infty}\end{array}\right) \\ \end{array}
$$

where  $\mathbf{\upsilon}_t^\infty$  is the limit of  $\mathbf{\upsilon}_t$  defined in (a.15). this shows also that  $\mathbf{\upsilon}_t^\infty$  is invertible. then applying proposition e.4 and  $(\mathbf{r}_1,\dots ,\mathbf{r}_t,\pmb {\lambda})\rightarrow (r_1,\dots ,r_t,\lambda)$  by  $t^{(b)}$ , we have

$$
\mathbf {r} _ {\parallel} \stackrel {{w}} {{\to}} r _ {\parallel} = \left( \begin{array}{c c c c c c} r _ {1} & \dots & r _ {t} & \lambda r _ {1} & \dots & \lambda r _ {t} \end{array} \right) (\mathbf {\upsilon} _ {t} ^ {\infty}) ^ {- 1} \left( \begin{array}{c} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {t} ^ {\infty} \\ \boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} ^ {\infty} \end{array} \right).
$$

for  $\mathbf{r}_{\perp}$ , observe that

$$
\begin{array}{l} n ^ {- 1} \left\| \pi_ {(\mathbf {u} _ {t}, \mathbf {z} _ {t}) ^ {\perp}} ^ {\top} \mathbf {u} _ {t + 1} \right\| ^ {2} \\ = n ^ {- 1} \| \mathbf {u} _ {t + 1} \| ^ {2} - n ^ {- 1} \mathbf {u} _ {t + 1} ^ {\top} \left( \begin{array}{c c} \mathbf {u} _ {t} & \mathbf {z} _ {t} \end{array} \right) \cdot \mathbf {m} _ {t} ^ {- 1} \cdot n ^ {- 1} \left( \begin{array}{c} \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\top} \\ \mathbf {z} _ {t} ^ {\top} \end{array} \right) \mathbf {u} _ {t + 1} \\ \rightarrow \mathbb {e} \left[ u _ {t + 1} ^ {2} \right] - \left(\left(\begin{array}{c}\boldsymbol {\delta} _ {t}\\\boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {t} \boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t}\end{array}\right) ^ {\top} \left(\begin{array}{c c}\boldsymbol {\delta} _ {t}&\boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {t}\\\boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {t} \boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} ^ {\top}&\boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {t}\end{array}\right) ^ {- 1} \left(\begin{array}{c}\boldsymbol {\delta} _ {t}\\\boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {t} \boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t}\end{array}\right)\right) ^ {\infty} \tag {a.22} \\ \end{array}
$$

where we use the condensed notation  $(\dots)^{\infty}$  to indicate that all quantities in the parentheses are evaluated at their  $n\to \infty$  limits. then by proposition f.2,

$$
\mathbf {r} _ {\perp} \stackrel {{w}} {{\to}} r _ {\perp} \sim \mathcal {n} \left(0, \mathbb {e} [ u _ {t + 1} ^ {2} ] - \left(\left( \begin{array}{c} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {t} \\ \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {t} \boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} \end{array} \right) ^ {\top} \left( \begin{array}{c c} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {t} & \boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {t} \\ \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {t} \boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} ^ {\top} & \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {t} \end{array} \right) ^ {- 1} \left( \begin{array}{c} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {t} \\ \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {t} \boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} \end{array} \right)\right) ^ {\infty}\right),
$$

where this limit  $r_{\perp}$  is independent of  $(r_1,\ldots ,r_t,\lambda)$ . combining these, we have  $(\mathbf{r}_1,\dots ,\mathbf{r}_{t + 1},\boldsymbol {\lambda})\stackrel {w}{\to}(r_1,\dots ,r_{t + 1},\lambda)$  where

$$
r _ {t + 1} = \left( \begin{array}{l l l l l l} r _ {1} & \dots & r _ {t} & \lambda r _ {1} & \dots & \lambda r _ {t} \end{array} \right) \left(\boldsymbol {\upsilon} _ {t} ^ {\infty}\right) ^ {- 1} \binom {\boldsymbol {\delta} _ {t} ^ {\infty}} {\boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} ^ {\infty}} + r _ {\perp}. \tag {a.23}
$$

we will require later in the argument that  $\operatorname{var}[r_{\perp}]$  given by (a.22) is strictly positive. let us verify this here: identifying the entries of

$$
\boldsymbol {\delta} _ {t} ^ {\infty}, \qquad \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {t} ^ {\infty} \boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} ^ {\infty}, \qquad \text {a n d} \qquad \mathbf {m} _ {t} ^ {\infty}
$$

as the quantities  $\mathbb{e}[u_su_{s'}]$ ,  $\mathbb{e}[z_su_{s'}]$ , and  $\mathbb{e}[z_sz_{s'}]$  for indices  $1\leq s,s'\leq t + 1$ , observe that this variance of  $r_{\perp}$  given by (a.22) is the variance of the residual of the projection of  $u_{t + 1}$  onto the linear span of the random variables  $(z_{1},\ldots ,z_{t},u_{1},\ldots ,u_{t})$  with respect to the  $l_{2}$ -inner-product  $(x,y)\mapsto \mathbb{e}[xy]$ . thus if  $\mathrm{var}[r_{\perp}] = 0$ , then there would exist scalar constants  $\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{t},\beta_{1},\dots,\beta_{t}$  such that

$$
u _ {t + 1} = \alpha_ {1} z _ {1} + \dots + \alpha_ {t} z _ {t} + \beta_ {1} u _ {1} + \dots + \beta_ {t} u _ {t}
$$

almost surely, but this contradicts assumption 4.2(e). so

$$
\operatorname {v a r} \left[ r _ {\perp} \right] > 0. \tag {a.24}
$$

let us now introduce a block notation for  $\mathbf{l}_{t + 1}^{(k,\infty)}$  (with blocks of sizes  $t$  and 1) given by

$$
\mathbf {l} _ {t + 1} ^ {(k, \infty)} = \left( \begin{array}{l l} \mathbf {l} _ {t} ^ {(k, \infty)} & \mathbf {l} _ {t} ^ {(k, \infty)} \\ (\mathbf {l} _ {t} ^ {(k, \infty)}) ^ {\top} & l _ {t + 1, t + 1} ^ {(k, \infty)} \end{array} \right). \tag {a.25}
$$

to conclude the proof of  $t + 1^{(b)}$ , it remains to compute the two quantities

$$
\mathbb {e} \left[ \left( \begin{array}{c c c} r _ {1} & \dots & r _ {t} \end{array} \right) ^ {\top} \lambda^ {k} r _ {t + 1} \right] \qquad \text {a n d} \qquad \mathbb {e} [ \lambda^ {k} r _ {t + 1} ^ {2} ]
$$

and show that they are given by  $\mathbf{l}_t^{(k,\infty)}$  and  $l_{t + 1,t + 1}^{(k,\infty)}$ .

for the first quantity, observe that  $\mathbb{e}[r_s\lambda^k r_\perp ] = 0$  for all  $s\leq t$ , because  $r_{\perp}$  has mean 0 and is independent of  $(r_s,\lambda)$ . then applying (a.23) and  $t^{(b)}$ ,

$$
\mathbb {e} \left[ \left( \begin{array}{c c c} r _ {1} & \dots & r _ {t} \end{array} \right) ^ {\top} \lambda^ {k} r _ {t + 1} \right] = \left(\left( \begin{array}{c c} \mathbf {l} _ {t} ^ {(k)} & \mathbf {l} _ {t} ^ {(k + 1)} \end{array} \right) \boldsymbol {\upsilon} _ {t} ^ {- 1} \left( \begin{array}{c} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {t} \\ \boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} \end{array} \right)\right) ^ {\infty}.
$$

applying the identity (a.16), we get

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbb {e} \left[ \left( \begin{array}{c c c} r _ {1} & \dots & r _ {t} \end{array} \right) ^ {\top} \lambda^ {k} r _ {t + 1} \right] = \left(\sum_ {j = 0} ^ {\infty} c _ {k, j} \phi_ {t} ^ {j} \pmb {\delta} _ {t} + \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {\infty} c _ {k, j + 1} \theta_ {t} ^ {(j)} \pmb {\phi} _ {t}\right) ^ {\infty} \\ = \left(\sum_ {j = 0} ^ {\infty} c _ {k, j} \phi_ {t + 1} ^ {j} \pmb {\delta} _ {t + 1} + \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {\infty} c _ {k, j + 1} \theta_ {t + 1} ^ {(j)} \phi_ {t + 1} ^ {\top}\right) _ {1: t, t + 1} ^ {\infty}. \\ \end{array}
$$

here, we use the notation  $(\cdot)_{1:t,t+1}$  to indicate the entries of rows 1 to  $t$  of column  $t+1$ . this last equality holds by writing  $\phi_{t+1}^{j}\delta_{t+1}$  and  $\theta_{t+1}^{(j)}\phi_{t+1}^{\top}$  in block form, and noting that we have the blocks

$$
\boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t + 1} ^ {j} = \left( \begin{array}{c c} \boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} ^ {j} & 0 \\ * & * \end{array} \right), \qquad \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {t + 1} = \left( \begin{array}{c c} * & \boldsymbol {\delta} \\ * & * \end{array} \right), \qquad \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {t + 1} ^ {(j)} = \left( \begin{array}{c c} \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {t} ^ {(j)} & * \\ * & * \end{array} \right), \qquad \boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t + 1} ^ {\top} = \left( \begin{array}{c c} * & \boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} \\ * & 0 \end{array} \right).
$$

finally, from the definitions of  $\theta_{t + 1}^{(j)}$  and  $\mathbf{l}_{t + 1}^{(k)}$ , the above is simply

$$
\mathbb {e} \left[ \left( \begin{array}{c c c} r _ {1} & \dots & r _ {t} \end{array} \right) ^ {\top} \lambda^ {k} r _ {t + 1} \right] = (\mathbf {l} _ {t + 1} ^ {(k, \infty)}) _ {1: t, t + 1} = \mathbf {l} _ {t} ^ {(k, \infty)}.
$$

for  $\mathbb{e}[\lambda^k r_{t + 1}^2]$ , we again apply (a.23) and the independence of  $r_{\perp}$  and  $(r_1,\ldots ,r_t,\lambda)$  to obtain similarly

$$
\mathbb {e} \left[ \lambda^ {k} r _ {t + 1} ^ {2} \right] = \left(\binom {\boldsymbol {\delta} _ {t}} {\boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t}} ^ {\top} \left(\boldsymbol {\upsilon} _ {t} ^ {- 1}\right) ^ {\top} \binom {\mathbf {l} _ {t} ^ {(k)}} {\mathbf {l} _ {t} ^ {(k + 1)}} \binom {\mathbf {l} _ {t} ^ {(k + 1)}} {\mathbf {l} _ {t} ^ {(k + 2)}} \boldsymbol {\upsilon} _ {t} ^ {- 1} \binom {\boldsymbol {\delta} _ {t}} {\boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t}}\right) ^ {\infty} + \mathbb {e} \left[ \lambda^ {k} r _ {\perp} ^ {2} \right]. \tag {a.26}
$$

applying independence of  $\lambda$  and  $r_{\perp}$  and taking the expected square on both sides of (a.23), we also have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbb {e} \left[ \lambda^ {k} r _ {\perp} ^ {2} \right] = m _ {k} ^ {\infty} \mathbb {e} \left[ r _ {\perp} ^ {2} \right] \\ = m _ {k} ^ {\infty} \left(\mathbb {e} \left[ r _ {t + 1} ^ {2} \right] - \mathbb {e} \left[ \left(\left(r _ {1} \quad \dots \quad r _ {t} \quad \lambda r _ {1} \quad \dots \quad \lambda r _ {t}\right) \left(\boldsymbol {\upsilon} _ {t} ^ {\infty}\right) ^ {- 1} \left( \begin{array}{c} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {t} ^ {\infty} \\ \boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} ^ {\infty} \end{array} \right)\right) ^ {2} \right]\right) \\ = c _ {k, 0} ^ {\infty} \left(\mathbb {e} [ u _ {t + 1} ^ {2} ] - \left(\left( \begin{array}{c} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {t} \\ \boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} \end{array} \right) ^ {\top} (\boldsymbol {\upsilon} _ {t} ^ {- 1}) ^ {\top} \left( \begin{array}{c c} \boldsymbol {\mathbf {l}} _ {t} ^ {(0)} & \boldsymbol {\mathbf {l}} _ {t} ^ {(1)} \\ \boldsymbol {\mathbf {l}} _ {t} ^ {(1)} & \boldsymbol {\mathbf {l}} _ {t} ^ {(2)} \end{array} \right) \boldsymbol {\upsilon} _ {t} ^ {- 1} \left( \begin{array}{c} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {t} \\ \boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} \end{array} \right)\right) ^ {\infty}\right), \\ \end{array}
$$

the last line identifying  $m_k^\infty = c_{k,0}^\infty$  by lemma a.1 and using

$$
\mathbb {e} [ r _ {t + 1} ^ {2} ] = \lim _ {n \to \infty} n ^ {- 1} \| \mathbf {r} _ {t + 1} \| ^ {2} = \lim _ {n \to \infty} n ^ {- 1} \| \mathbf {u} _ {t + 1} \| ^ {2} = \mathbb {e} [ u _ {t + 1} ^ {2} ].
$$

applying this to (a.26), and then applying the identity (a.17), we get

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbb {e} [ \lambda^ {k} r _ {t + 1} ^ {2} ] = c _ {k, 0} ^ {\infty} \mathbb {e} [ u _ {t + 1} ^ {2} ] + \left(\left( \begin{array}{c} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {t} \\ \boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} \end{array} \right) ^ {\top} \left( \begin{array}{c c} 0 & \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {\infty} c _ {k, j + 1} (\boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} ^ {j}) ^ {\top} \\ \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {\infty} c _ {k, j + 1} \boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} ^ {j} & \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {\infty} c _ {k, j + 2} \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {t} ^ {(j)} \end{array} \right) \left( \begin{array}{c} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {t} \\ \boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} \end{array} \right)\right) ^ {\infty} \\ = c _ {k, 0} ^ {\infty} \mathbb {e} [ u _ {t + 1} ^ {2} ] + \left(\sum_ {j = 0} ^ {\infty} c _ {k, j + 1} \pmb {\delta} _ {t} ^ {\top} (\pmb {\phi} _ {t} ^ {j}) ^ {\top} \pmb {\phi} _ {t} + c _ {k, j + 1} \pmb {\phi} _ {t} ^ {\top} \pmb {\phi} _ {t} ^ {j} \pmb {\delta} _ {t} + c _ {k, j + 2} \pmb {\phi} _ {t} ^ {\top} \pmb {\theta} _ {t} ^ {(j)} \pmb {\phi} _ {t}\right) ^ {\infty} \\ = \left(c _ {k, 0} \pmb {\delta} _ {t + 1} + \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {\infty} c _ {k, j + 1} \pmb {\delta} _ {t + 1} (\pmb {\phi} _ {t + 1} ^ {j + 1}) ^ {\top} + c _ {k, j + 1} \pmb {\phi} _ {t + 1} ^ {j + 1} \pmb {\delta} _ {t + 1} + c _ {k, j + 2} \pmb {\phi} _ {t + 1} \pmb {\theta} _ {t + 1} ^ {(j)} \pmb {\phi} _ {t + 1} ^ {\top}\right) _ {t + 1, t + 1} ^ {\infty}. \\ \end{array}
$$

here, we use  $(\cdot)_{t+1,t+1}$  to denote the lower-right entry, and this last equality follows again from writing the matrix products in block form and observing that

$$
\boldsymbol {\delta} _ {t + 1} = \left( \begin{array}{c c} * & \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {t} \\ \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {t} ^ {\top} & \mathbb {e} [ u _ {t + 1} ^ {2} ] \end{array} \right), \quad (\boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t + 1} ^ {j + 1}) ^ {\top} = \left( \begin{array}{c c} * & (\boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} ^ {j}) ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} \\ * & 0 \end{array} \right), \quad \boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t + 1} ^ {j + 1} = \left( \begin{array}{c c} * & * \\ \boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} ^ {j} & 0 \end{array} \right), \quad \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {t + 1} ^ {(j)} = \left( \begin{array}{c c} \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {t} ^ {(j)} & * \\ * & * \end{array} \right).
$$

applying the definitions of  $\theta_{t + 1}^{(j)}$  and  $\mathbf{l}_{t + 1}^{(k)}$ , this is just

$$
\mathbb {e} \left[ \lambda^ {k} r _ {t + 1} ^ {2} \right] = \left(\sum_ {j = 0} ^ {\infty} c _ {k, j} \theta_ {t + 1} ^ {(j)}\right) _ {t + 1, t + 1} ^ {\infty} = l _ {t + 1, t + 1} ^ {(k, \infty)}.
$$

this concludes the proof of  $t + 1^{(b)}$ .

step 3: analysis of  $\mathbf{z}_{t + 1}$ . assuming  $t + 1^{(a)}, t + 1^{(b)}, t^{(c)}, t^{(d)}$ , we now show  $t + 1^{(c)}$  and  $t + 1^{(d)}$ . define the  $(t + 1) \times t$  matrices

$$
\tilde {\boldsymbol {\phi}} _ {t} = \left( \begin{array}{l} \boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} \\ \boldsymbol {\phi} _ {t} ^ {\top} \end{array} \right), \quad \tilde {\mathbf {b}} _ {t} = \left( \begin{array}{l} \mathbf {b} _ {t} \\ \mathbf {0} \end{array} \right). \tag {a.27}
$$

these are the first  $t$  columns of  $\phi_{t + 1}$  and  $\mathbf{b}_{t + 1}$ , and we have  $\mathbf{r}_t\mathbf{b}_t = \mathbf{r}_{t + 1}\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_t$ . conditional on  $\mathbf{u}_t,\mathbf{r}_t,\mathbf{z}_t,\mathbf{u}_{t + 1},\mathbf{r}_{t + 1},\boldsymbol {\lambda},\mathbf{e}$ , the law of  $\mathbf{o}$  is conditioned on the event

$$
\left(\mathbf {r} _ {t + 1} \quad \boldsymbol {\lambda} \mathbf {r} _ {t}\right) \left( \begin{array}{l l} \operatorname {i d} & - \tilde {\mathbf {b}} _ {t} \\ 0 & \operatorname {i d} \end{array} \right) = \mathbf {o} \left(\mathbf {u} _ {t + 1} \quad \mathbf {z} _ {t}\right). \tag {a.28}
$$
