the inequalities in the preceding argument stem from (c.35) and (c.40). if both  $u_{*} \sim \mathcal{n}(0,1)$  and  $v_{*} \sim \mathcal{n}(0,1)$ , then (c.35) holds with equality. in this case, we have  $(\delta_{*} / \gamma_{*})\sigma_{\mathrm{lb}} = f(\sigma_{\mathrm{lb}},\omega_{\mathrm{lb}})$  and  $(\gamma_{*} / \delta_{*})\omega_{\mathrm{lb}} = g(\sigma_{\mathrm{lb}},\omega_{\mathrm{lb}})$  in the preceding argument, and these two equations may be solved to yield  $\delta_{*} = \delta_{\mathrm{pca}}$  and  $\gamma_{*} = \gamma_{\mathrm{pca}}$ . then equality holds in (3.32). (note that equality also holds in (c.40) because  $(1 + \gamma r(x))\delta_{\mathrm{pca}} = (1 + r(x))\gamma_{\mathrm{pca}}$  by (c.32).) conversely, if either  $u_{*}$  or  $v_{*}$  is not distributed as  $\mathcal{n}(0,1)$ , then at least one of the inequalities in (c.35) is strict. then the inequality (c.38) is also strict, implying that (3.32) holds with strict inequality as well.

# appendix d. removing the non-degeneracy assumption

in this appendix, we prove corollary 4.4. we follow a similar approach to [bmn20] and construct a perturbed version of the amp sequence: let  $\pmb{\gamma},\mathbf{w}_1,\mathbf{w}_2,\dots \in \mathbb{r}^n$  be random vectors independent of each other and of all other quantities, where  $\pmb{\gamma}$  has i.i.d.  $\mathrm{uniform}(-1,1)$  entries and each  $\mathbf{w}_t$  has i.i.d.  $\mathcal{n}(0,1)$  entries. for a fixed small parameter  $\varepsilon >0$ , consider the perturbed noise matrix

$$
\mathbf {w} ^ {\varepsilon} = \mathbf {o} ^ {\top} \operatorname {d i a g} (\boldsymbol {\lambda} + \varepsilon \boldsymbol {\gamma}) \mathbf {o},
$$

the perturbed initialization

$$
\mathbf {u} _ {1} ^ {\varepsilon} = \mathbf {u} _ {1} + \varepsilon \mathbf {w} _ {1}, \tag {d.1}
$$

and the perturbed amp iterations

$$
\mathbf {z} _ {t} ^ {\varepsilon} = \mathbf {w} ^ {\varepsilon} \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\varepsilon} - b _ {t 1} ^ {\varepsilon} \mathbf {u} _ {1} ^ {\varepsilon} - \dots - b _ {t t} ^ {\varepsilon} \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\varepsilon} \tag {d.2}
$$

$$
\mathbf {u} _ {t + 1} ^ {\varepsilon} = u _ {t + 1} \left(\mathbf {z} _ {1} ^ {\varepsilon}, \dots , \mathbf {z} _ {t} ^ {\varepsilon}, \mathbf {e}\right) + \varepsilon \mathbf {w} _ {t + 1}. \tag {d.3}
$$

we define  $\pmb{\delta}_{t}^{\varepsilon},\pmb{\phi}_{t}^{\varepsilon},\mathbf{b}_{t}^{\varepsilon},\pmb{\sigma}_{t}^{\varepsilon}$  by (4.4) and (4.7) using this perturbed sequence, and the above coefficients  $(b_{t1}^{\varepsilon},\dots,b_{tt}^{\varepsilon})$  are the last column of  $\mathbf{b}_t^\varepsilon$

note that for any fixed  $\varepsilon > 0$  and up to any fixed iteration  $t$ , these perturbed iterations are an example of the general iterations (4.2-4.3) applied with noise matrix  $\mathbf{w}^{\varepsilon}$ , by defining the augmented side-information matrix  $\mathbf{e}^{\varepsilon} = (\mathbf{e}, \mathbf{w}_2, \ldots, \mathbf{w}_{t+1})$  and considering the functions

$$
u _ {t + 1} ^ {\varepsilon} (\mathbf {z} _ {1}, \ldots , \mathbf {z} _ {t}, \mathbf {e} ^ {\varepsilon}) = u _ {t + 1} (\mathbf {z} _ {1}, \ldots , \mathbf {z} _ {t}, \mathbf {e}) + \varepsilon \mathbf {w} _ {t + 1}.
$$

by propositions e.1 and e.4, we have

$$
\boldsymbol {\lambda} + \varepsilon \boldsymbol {\gamma} \stackrel {w} {\to} \lambda^ {\varepsilon} \equiv \lambda + \varepsilon \gamma , \quad (\mathbf {u} _ {1} ^ {\varepsilon}, \mathbf {e}, \mathbf {w} _ {2}, \ldots , \mathbf {w} _ {t + 1}) \stackrel {w} {\to} (u _ {1} + \varepsilon w _ {1}, e, w _ {2}, \ldots , w _ {t + 1}),
$$

where  $\gamma \sim \mathrm{uniform}(-1,1)$  is independent of  $\lambda$  and  $(w_{1},\ldots ,w_{t + 1})\sim \mathcal{n}(0,\mathrm{id})$  is independent of  $(u_{1},e)$ . it is then clear that assumption 4.2 including part (e) holds for this perturbed sequence, so lemma a.4 applies.

define the almost-sure limits

$$
(\pmb {\delta} _ {t} ^ {\varepsilon , \infty}, \pmb {\phi} _ {t} ^ {\varepsilon , \infty}, \mathbf {b} _ {t} ^ {\varepsilon , \infty}, \pmb {\sigma} _ {t} ^ {\varepsilon , \infty}) = \lim _ {n \to \infty} (\pmb {\delta} _ {t} ^ {\varepsilon}, \pmb {\phi} _ {t} ^ {\varepsilon}, \mathbf {b} _ {t} ^ {\varepsilon}, \pmb {\sigma} _ {t} ^ {\varepsilon}),
$$

as guaranteed by lemma a.4. we let  $\mathbf{u}_1, \mathbf{z}_1, \mathbf{u}_2, \mathbf{z}_2, \ldots$  continue to denote the original amp sequence. we now establish inductively the following two claims, almost surely for each  $t = 1, 2, 3, \ldots$ , where the second claim implies the corollary:

(a)

$$
\limsup_{n\to \infty}n^{-1}\| \mathbf{z}_{t - 1}\|^{2} <   \infty ,\qquad \lim_{\varepsilon \to 0}\limsup_{n\to \infty}n^{-1}\| \mathbf{z}_{t - 1}^{\varepsilon} - \mathbf{z}_{t - 1}\|^{2} = 0,
$$

$$
\limsup_{n\to \infty}n^{-1}\| \mathbf{u}_{t}\|^{2} <   \infty ,\qquad \lim_{\varepsilon \to 0}\limsup_{n\to \infty}n^{-1}\| \mathbf{u}_{t}^{\varepsilon} - \mathbf{u}_{t}\|^{2} = 0.
$$

(b)  $(\mathbf{u}_1,\dots ,\mathbf{u}_t,\mathbf{z}_1,\dots ,\mathbf{z}_{t - 1},\mathbf{e})\stackrel {w_2}{\to} (u_1,\dots ,u_t,z_1,\dots ,z_{t - 1},e)$ . the deterministic limits

$$
(\pmb {\delta} _ {t} ^ {\infty}, \pmb {\phi} _ {t} ^ {\infty}, \mathbf {b} _ {t} ^ {\infty}, \pmb {\sigma} _ {t} ^ {\infty}) = \lim _ {n \to \infty} (\pmb {\delta} _ {t}, \pmb {\phi} _ {t}, \mathbf {b} _ {t}, \pmb {\sigma} _ {t})
$$

all exist, where  $(\pmb{\delta}_t^\infty, \pmb{\phi}_t^\infty, \mathbf{b}_t^\infty, \pmb{\sigma}_t^\infty) = \lim_{\varepsilon \to 0} (\pmb{\delta}_t^{\varepsilon,\infty}, \pmb{\phi}_t^{\varepsilon,\infty}, \mathbf{b}_t^{\varepsilon,\infty}, \pmb{\sigma}_t^{\varepsilon,\infty})$ .

let  $t^{(a)}, t^{(b)}$  denote these claims up to and including iteration  $t$ . we induct on  $t$ .

for  $1^{(a)}$ , we have  $n^{-1}\| \mathbf{u}_1\|^2 \to \mathbb{e}[u_1^2] < \infty$  by assumption 4.2(c). we also have  $\mathbf{u}_1^\varepsilon - \mathbf{u}_1 = \varepsilon \mathbf{w}_1$  and  $n^{-1}\| \mathbf{w}_1\|^2 \to 1$ . thus  $\lim_{\varepsilon \to 0} \lim_{n \to \infty} n^{-1}\| \mathbf{u}_1^\varepsilon - \mathbf{u}_1\|^2 = 0$ .

for  $1^{(b)}$ , we have  $(\mathbf{u}_1, \mathbf{e}) \xrightarrow{w_2} (u_1, e)$  and  $\pmb{\delta}_{1} \to \mathbb{e}[u_{1}^{2}]$  also by assumption 4.2(c). since  $\phi_{1} = 0$  and  $\kappa_{k} \to \kappa_{k}^{\infty}$  (the  $k^{\mathrm{th}}$  free cumulant of  $\lambda$ ) for each  $k \geq 1$ , this shows the existence of all four limits  $\pmb{\delta}_{1}^{\infty}, \pmb{\phi}_{1}^{\infty}, \pmb{\mathbf{b}}_{1}^{\infty}, \pmb{\sigma}_{1}^{\infty}$ . note that  $\pmb{\delta}_{1}^{\varepsilon,\infty} = \mathbb{e}[u_{1}^{2}] + \varepsilon^{2}$ , so that  $\pmb{\delta}_{1}^{\varepsilon,\infty} \to \pmb{\delta}_{1} = \mathbb{e}[u_{1}^{2}]$  as  $\varepsilon \to 0$ . letting  $\kappa_{k}^{\varepsilon,\infty}$  be the free cumulants of  $\lambda^{\varepsilon}$ , note that the moments of  $\lambda^{\varepsilon}$  converge to those of  $\lambda$  as  $\varepsilon \to 0$ , so also  $\kappa_{k}^{\varepsilon,\infty} \to \kappa_{k}^{\infty}$ . since  $\phi_{1}^{\varepsilon,\infty} = 0 = \phi_{1}$ , this shows the last statement of  $1^{(b)}$ .

suppose now that  $t^{(a)}$  and  $t^{(b)}$  hold. to show  $t + 1^{(a)}$ , observe that

$$
\| \mathbf {z} _ {t} \| \leq \| \mathbf {w} \| \| \mathbf {u} _ {t} \| + \sum_ {s = 1} ^ {t} | b _ {t s} | \| \mathbf {u} _ {s} \|.
$$

applying  $\| \mathbf{w}\| = \| \pmb {\lambda}\|_{\infty}$  , lim supn->∞ ||a||o <, and lim supn->o n-1||u|2 < o and limn->o |bts| =  $|b_{ts}^{\infty}|$  by  $t^{(a)}$  and  $t^{(b)}$  , this shows

$$
\limsup_{n\to \infty}n^{-1}\| \mathbf{z}_{t}\|^{2} <   \infty .
$$

now comparing (4.2) with (d.2),

$$
\| \mathbf {z} _ {t} ^ {\varepsilon} - \mathbf {z} _ {t} \| \leq \| \mathbf {w} ^ {\varepsilon} - \mathbf {w} \| \| \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\varepsilon} \| + \| \mathbf {w} \| \| \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\varepsilon} - \mathbf {u} _ {t} \| + \sum_ {s = 1} ^ {t} | b _ {t s} ^ {\varepsilon} - b _ {t s} | \| \mathbf {u} _ {s} ^ {\varepsilon} \| + | b _ {t s} | \| \mathbf {u} _ {s} ^ {\varepsilon} - \mathbf {u} _ {s} \|.
$$

applying also  $\| \mathbf{w}^{\varepsilon} - \mathbf{w}\| \leq \varepsilon$ ,  $\lim_{\varepsilon \to 0}|b_{ts}^{\varepsilon}| = |b_{ts}|$ , and  $\lim_{\varepsilon \to 0}\lim \sup_{n\to \infty}n^{-1}\| \mathbf{u}_s^\varepsilon -\mathbf{u}_s\| ^2 = 0$  by  $t^{(a)}$  and  $t^{(b)}$ , this shows

$$
\lim  _ {\varepsilon \rightarrow 0} \lim  _ {n \rightarrow \infty} n ^ {- 1} \left\| \mathbf {z} _ {t} ^ {\varepsilon} - \mathbf {z} _ {t} \right\| ^ {2} = 0. \tag {d.4}
$$

for  $\mathbf{u}_{t + 1}$ , we have

$$
n ^ {- 1} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \left(u _ {t + 1} \left(z _ {i 1}, \dots , z _ {i t}, e\right) - u _ {t + 1} (0, \dots , 0, e)\right) ^ {2} \leq c n ^ {- 1} \left(\left\| \mathbf {z} _ {1} \right\| ^ {2} + \dots + \left\| \mathbf {z} _ {t} \right\| ^ {2}\right)
$$

by the lipschitz assumption for  $u_{t + 1}$ . then applying  $t^{(a)}$  to bound the right side,

$$
\operatorname * {l i m s u p} _ {n \to \infty} n ^ {- 1} \| \mathbf {u} _ {t + 1} \| ^ {2} <   \infty .
$$

now comparing (4.3) and (d.3),

$$
\begin{array}{l} n ^ {- 1} \| \mathbf {u} _ {t + 1} ^ {\varepsilon} - \mathbf {u} _ {t + 1} \| ^ {2} \leq 2 \varepsilon^ {2} \cdot n ^ {- 1} \| \mathbf {w} _ {t + 1} \| ^ {2} + 2 n ^ {- 1} \| u _ {t + 1} (\mathbf {z} _ {1}, \ldots , \mathbf {z} _ {t}, \mathbf {e}) - u _ {t + 1} (\mathbf {z} _ {1} ^ {\varepsilon}, \ldots , \mathbf {z} _ {t} ^ {\varepsilon}, \mathbf {e}) \| ^ {2} \\ \leq 2 \varepsilon^ {2} \cdot n ^ {- 1} \| \mathbf {w} _ {t + 1} \| ^ {2} + 2 c \sum_ {s = 1} ^ {t} n ^ {- 1} \| \mathbf {z} _ {s} ^ {\varepsilon} - \mathbf {z} _ {s} \| ^ {2}. \\ \end{array}
$$

then applying  $t^{(a)}$  and (d.4) to bound the right side, we obtain

$$
\lim _ {\varepsilon \to 0} \lim _ {n \to \infty} \sup _ {n \to \infty} n ^ {- 1} \| \mathbf {u} _ {t + 1} ^ {\varepsilon} - \mathbf {u} _ {t + 1} \| ^ {2} = 0.
$$

this shows  $t + 1^{(a)}$ .

for  $t + 1^{(b)}$ , let

$$
x _ {i} = \left(\mathbf {u} _ {1}, \dots , \mathbf {u} _ {t + 1}, \mathbf {z} _ {1}, \dots , \mathbf {z} _ {t}, \mathbf {e}\right) _ {i} \tag {d.5}
$$

$$
x _ {i} ^ {\varepsilon} = \left(\mathbf {u} _ {1} ^ {\varepsilon}, \dots , \mathbf {u} _ {t + 1} ^ {\varepsilon}, \mathbf {z} _ {1} ^ {\varepsilon}, \dots , \mathbf {z} _ {t} ^ {\varepsilon}, \mathbf {e}\right) _ {i} \tag {d.6}
$$

be the  $i^{\mathrm{th}}$  rows of these matrices. let  $x = (u_{1},\ldots ,u_{t + 1},z_{1},\ldots ,z_{t},e)$  be the limit to be shown, and let  $x^{\varepsilon}$  be the limit of the perturbed sequence. to show the desired  $w_{2}$  convergence, it suffices to check the convergence

$$
\frac {1}{n} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} f \left(x _ {i}\right)\rightarrow \mathbb {e} [ f (x) ] \tag {d.7}
$$

for all lipschitz functions  $f(x)$  and for  $f(x) = \| x\| ^2$ . let us write

$$
\begin{array}{l} \limsup_{n\to \infty}\left|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i = 1}^{n}f(x_{i}) - \mathbb{e}[f(x)]\right| \\ \leq \lim  _ {\varepsilon \rightarrow 0} \lim  _ {n \rightarrow \infty} \sup  _ {n \rightarrow \infty} \left(\left| \frac {1}{n} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} f \left(x _ {i}\right) - f \left(x _ {i} ^ {\varepsilon}\right)\right| + \left| \frac {1}{n} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} f \left(x _ {i} ^ {\varepsilon}\right) - \mathbb {e} [ f (x ^ {\varepsilon}) ] \right| + \left| \mathbb {e} [ f (x ^ {\varepsilon}) ] - \mathbb {e} [ f (x) ] \right|\right). \\ \end{array}
$$

for the first term of (d.8), note that any such function  $f$  satisfies the pseudo-lipschitz condition

$$
| f (x) - f (x ^ {\prime}) | \leq c (1 + \| x \| + \| x ^ {\prime} \|) \| x - x ^ {\prime} \|
$$

for some constant  $c > 0$ . then by this and cauchy-schwarz,

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left| \frac {1}{n} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} f (x _ {i}) - f (x _ {i} ^ {\varepsilon}) \right| \leq \frac {c}{n} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} (1 + \| x _ {i} \| + \| x _ {i} ^ {\varepsilon} \|) \| x _ {i} - x _ {i} ^ {\varepsilon} \| \\ \leq c ^ {\prime} \left(\frac {1}{n} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} (1 + \| x _ {i} \| ^ {2} + \| x _ {i} ^ {\varepsilon} \| ^ {2})\right) ^ {1 / 2} \left(\frac {1}{n} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \| x _ {i} - x _ {i} ^ {\varepsilon} \| ^ {2}\right) ^ {1 / 2}. \\ \end{array}
$$

recalling the definitions of  $x_{i}$  and  $x_{i}^{\varepsilon}$  in (d.5) and (d.6) and applying  $t + 1^{(a)}$ , this term converges to 0 in the limits  $n\to \infty$  followed by  $\varepsilon \rightarrow 0$ . the second term of (d.8) converges to 0 as  $n\to \infty$  for any fixed  $\varepsilon >0$ , by lemma a.4. for the third term of (d.8), note that as  $\varepsilon \rightarrow 0$ , we have

$$
u _ {1} ^ {\varepsilon} \rightarrow u _ {1}, \quad \left(z _ {1} ^ {\varepsilon}, \dots , z _ {t} ^ {\varepsilon}\right)\rightarrow \left(z _ {1}, \dots , z _ {t}\right)
$$

in the wasserstein space  $w_{2}$ , where the second convergence follows from  $\| \pmb{\sigma}_t^\varepsilon - \pmb{\sigma}_t\| \to 0$  in  $t^{(b)}$ . since the functions  $u_{2},\ldots ,u_{t + 1}$  are lipschitz, this implies  $x^{\varepsilon}\rightarrow x$  in  $w_{2}$ , so  $\mathbb{e}[f(x^{\varepsilon})]\to \mathbb{e}[f(x)]$ . combining these establishes (d.7), and hence

$$
\left(\mathbf {u} _ {1}, \dots , \mathbf {u} _ {t + 1}, \mathbf {z} _ {1}, \dots , \mathbf {z} _ {t}, \mathbf {e}\right) \stackrel {{w _ {2}}} {{\rightarrow}} \left(u _ {1}, \dots , u _ {t + 1}, z _ {1}, \dots , z _ {t}, e\right).
$$

this implies the existence of the limits  $\delta_{t + 1}^{\infty}$ . each function  $u_{s}$  is lipschitz and continuously differentiable by assumption, so each partial derivative  $\partial_{s'} u_{s}$  is bounded and continuous. then this

also implies the existence of  $\phi_{t + 1}^{\infty}$ , and hence of  $\mathbf{b}_{t + 1}^{\infty}$  and  $\pmb{\sigma}_{t + 1}^{\infty}$ . as  $\varepsilon \to 0$ , since  $x^{\varepsilon} \to x$  in  $w_{2}$  as shown above, we also have  $\pmb{\delta}_{t + 1}^{\varepsilon, \infty} \to \pmb{\delta}_{t + 1}^{\infty}$  and  $\phi_{t + 1}^{\varepsilon, \infty} \to \phi_{t + 1}^{\infty}$ , and hence  $\mathbf{b}_{t + 1}^{\varepsilon, \infty} \to \mathbf{b}_{t + 1}^{\infty}$  and  $\pmb{\sigma}_{t + 1}^{\varepsilon, \infty} \to \pmb{\sigma}_{t + 1}^{\infty}$ . this concludes the proof of  $t + 1^{(b)}$ .

# appendix e. properties of empirical wasserstein convergence

in this appendix, we collect several properties of the notation  $\xrightarrow{w}$  and  $\xrightarrow{w_p}$  for empirical wasserstein convergence from section 2.2.

we will use below the following fact: to verify  $\mathbf{v} \xrightarrow{w_p} \mathcal{l}$  where  $\mathbf{v} \in \mathbb{r}^{n \times k}$ , it suffices to check that (2.2) holds for every function  $f: \mathbb{r}^k \to \mathbb{r}$  satisfying, for some constant  $c > 0$ , the pseudo-lipschitz condition

$$
| f (v) - f \left(v ^ {\prime}\right) | \leq c \left(1 + \| v \| ^ {p - 1} + \| v ^ {\prime} \| ^ {p - 1}\right) \| v - v ^ {\prime} \|. \tag {e.1}
$$

this is because by [vil08, definition 6.7], it suffices to check (2.2) for  $f(v) = \| v\| ^p$ , together with the usual weak convergence which is equivalent to (2.2) holding for bounded lipschitz functions. note that this condition (e.1) implies the polynomial growth condition (2.1).

proposition e.1. fix any  $p \geq 1$ ,  $t \geq 1$ , and  $k \geq 0$ . let  $\mathbf{e} \in \mathbb{r}^{n \times k}$  be a deterministic matrix satisfying  $\mathbf{e} \xrightarrow{w_p} e$ , and let  $\mathbf{v} \in \mathbb{r}^{n \times t}$  be random with i.i.d. rows equal in law to  $v \in \mathbb{r}^t$ , where  $\mathbb{e}[\| v\|^p] < \infty$ . then the joint convergence

$$
(\mathbf {v}, \mathbf {e}) \stackrel {{w _ {p}}} {{\to}} (v, e)
$$

holds almost surely, where  $v$  is independent of  $e$  in the limit  $(v,e)$ .

proof. for  $k = 0$ , the result  $\mathbf{v} \xrightarrow{w_p} v$  follows from the strong law of large numbers applied to any function  $f$  satisfying (2.1), as  $\mathbb{e}[|f(v)|] \leq c(1 + \mathbb{e}[\|v\|^p]) < \infty$ .

for  $k > 0$ , we proceed by approximating  $e$  with a discrete random variable, and then applying the law of large numbers for each discrete value of  $e$ . in detail: fix any function  $f$  satisfying (e.1), and fix any  $\varepsilon \in (0,1)$ . let  $\{(v_i, e_i)\}_{i=1}^n$  be the rows of  $(\mathbf{v}, \mathbf{e})$ . since (e.1) implies (2.1), for any  $r > 0$  we have

$$
\frac {1}{n} \sum_ {i: \| e _ {i} \| > r} | f (v _ {i}, e _ {i}) | \leq \frac {c}{n} \sum_ {i: \| e _ {i} \| > r} \left(1 + \| v _ {i} \| ^ {p} + \| e _ {i} \| ^ {p}\right). \tag {e.2}
$$

note that as  $\mathbf{e} \stackrel{w_p}{\to} e$ , the uniform integrability condition of [vil08, definition 6.7(iii)] shows

$$
\lim _ {r \to \infty} \lim _ {n \to \infty} \sup _ {i: \| e _ {i} \| > r} \frac {1}{n} \sum_ {i: \| e _ {i} \| > r} 1 \leq \lim _ {r \to \infty} \lim _ {n \to \infty} \sup _ {i: \| e _ {i} \| > r} \frac {1}{n} \sum_ {i: \| e _ {i} \| > r} \| e _ {i} \| ^ {p} = 0.
$$

this bounds the first and third terms on the right side of (e.2). for the middle term, we consider two cases. if  $\| v \| \leq k$  almost surely for some  $k > 0$ , then by this and the convergence  $\mathbf{v} \stackrel{w_p}{\to} v$ ,

$$
\lim _ {n \to \infty} \frac {1}{n} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \min (\| v _ {i} \|, 2 k) ^ {p} = \mathbb {e} [ \min (\| v \|, 2 k) ^ {p} ] = \mathbb {e} [ \| v \| ^ {p} ] = \lim _ {n \to \infty} \frac {1}{n} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \| v _ {i} \| ^ {p}.
$$

applying also  $|\{i: \| v_i\| > 2k\}| / n \to 0$ , this implies

$$
\lim _ {n \to \infty} \frac {1}{n} \sum_ {i: \| v _ {i} \| > 2 k} \| v _ {i} \| ^ {p} = \lim _ {n \to \infty} \left(\frac {1}{n} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} (\| v _ {i} \| ^ {p} - \min (\| v _ {i} \|, 2 k) ^ {p}) + (2 k) ^ {p} \cdot \frac {| \{i : \| v _ {i} \| > 2 k \} |}{n}\right) = 0.
$$

in this case, we may bound

$$
\lim  _ {r \to \infty} \lim  _ {n \to \infty} \frac {1}{n} \sum_ {i: \| e _ {i} \| > r} \| v _ {i} \| ^ {p} \leq \lim  _ {r \to \infty} \lim  _ {n \to \infty} \sup  _ {r} \frac {1}{n} \left(\sum_ {i: \| e _ {i} \| > r} (2 k) ^ {p} + \sum_ {i: \| v _ {i} \| > 2 k} \| v _ {i} \| ^ {p}\right) = 0.
$$

conversely, if the support of  $v$  is unbounded, then let  $\| v \|_{(1)} \geq \ldots \geq \| v \|_{(n)}$  be the ordered values of  $\{\| v_i \| \}_{i=1}^n$ . note that for each  $r > 0$ , we have  $|\{i : \| e_i \| > r\}| / n \to \delta(r)$  for some  $\delta(r) \geq 0$  where  $\delta(r) \to 0$  as  $r \to \infty$ . then

$$
\lim _ {r \to \infty} \lim _ {n \to \infty} \sup _ {i: \| e _ {i} \| > r} \frac {1}{n} \sum_ {i: \| e _ {i} \| > r} \| v _ {i} \| ^ {p} \leq \lim _ {\delta \to 0} \lim _ {n \to \infty} \sup _ {i = 1} \frac {1}{n} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {\delta n} \| v \| _ {(i)} ^ {p}.
$$

now applying  $\mathbf{v} \xrightarrow{w_p} v$  and the corresponding uniform integrability condition for  $\mathbf{v}$ ,

$$
\lim _ {\delta \to 0} \lim _ {n \to \infty} \sup _ {n \to \infty} \frac {1}{n} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {\delta n} \| v \| _ {(i)} ^ {p} = \lim _ {r \to \infty} \lim _ {n \to \infty} \sup _ {n \to \infty} \frac {1}{n} \sum_ {i: \| v _ {i} \| > r} \| v _ {i} \| ^ {p} = 0.
$$

combining the above and applying this to (e.2),

$$
\lim _ {r \to \infty} \lim _ {n \to \infty} \sup _ {n \to \infty} \frac {1}{n} \sum_ {i: \| e _ {i} \| > r} | f (v _ {i}, e _ {i}) | = 0.
$$

so we may pick a bounded set  $\mathcal{b} \subset \mathbb{r}^k$  large enough such that

$$
\lim  _ {n \rightarrow \infty} \frac {1}{n} \sum_ {i: e _ {i} \notin \mathcal {b}} | f (v _ {i}, e _ {i}) | <   \varepsilon . \tag {e.3}
$$

applying also

$$
\mathbb {e} \big [ | f (v, e) | \cdot \mathbf {1} \{e \notin \mathcal {b} \} \big ] \leq \mathbb {e} \big [ c (1 + \| v \| ^ {p} + \| e \| ^ {p}) \mathbf {1} \{e \notin \mathcal {b} \} \big ]
$$

and the integrability of  $\| v\| ^p$  and  $\| e\| ^p$ , we may pick  $\mathcal{b}$  large enough such that

$$
\mathbb {e} \left[ | f (v, e) | \cdot \mathbf {1} \{e \notin \mathcal {b} \} \right] <   \varepsilon . \tag {e.4}
$$

now let  $\{u_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha = 1}^{m}$  be any finite partition of  $\mathcal{b}$  such that each set  $u_{\alpha}$  has diameter at most  $\varepsilon$  and the boundary of  $u_{\alpha}$  has probability 0 under the law of  $e$ . (for example, take  $\mathcal{b} = [-k,k]^k$  to be a hyperrectangle in  $\mathbb{r}^k$ , and construct this partition by dividing  $[-k,k]$  along each axis into small enough intervals. take  $-k$ ,  $k$ , and these interval boundaries to have probability 0 under the univariate marginal distribution of each coordinate of  $e$ .) pick a point  $u_{\alpha} \in u_{\alpha}$  for each  $\alpha = 1,\ldots,m$ . for each  $e \in \mathcal{b}$ , define  $u(e) = u_{\alpha}$  where  $\alpha$  is the index such that  $e \in u_{\alpha}$ . then applying (e.1) and  $\|u(e)\|^{p - 1} \leq c(\|e\|^{p - 1} + 1)$ ,

$$
\frac {1}{n} \sum_ {i: e _ {i} \in \mathcal {b}} | f (v _ {i}, e _ {i}) - f (v _ {i}, u (e _ {i})) | \leq \frac {c}{n} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \left(1 + \| v _ {i} \| ^ {p - 1} + \| e _ {i} \| ^ {p - 1}\right) \cdot \varepsilon
$$

for a constant  $c > 0$  independent of  $\varepsilon$ . since  $\mathbf{v} \xrightarrow{w_p} v$  and  $\mathbf{e} \xrightarrow{w_p} e$ , this yields

$$
\lim  _ {n \rightarrow \infty} \sup  _ {n \rightarrow \infty} \frac {1}{n} \sum_ {i: e _ {i} \in \mathcal {b}} | f (v _ {i}, e _ {i}) - f (v _ {i}, u (e _ {i})) | \leq c ^ {\prime} \varepsilon . \tag {e.5}
$$

similarly,

$$
\mathbb {e} \left[ | f (v, e) - f (v, u (e)) | \cdot \mathbf {1} \{e \in \mathcal {b} \} \right] \leq c ^ {\prime} \varepsilon . \tag {e.6}
$$

finally, let us write

$$
\frac {1}{n} \sum_ {i: e _ {i} \in \mathcal {b}} f (v _ {i}, u (e _ {i})) = \sum_ {\alpha = 1} ^ {m} \frac {1}{n} \sum_ {i: e _ {i} \in u _ {\alpha}} f (v _ {i}, u _ {\alpha}).
$$

observe that for each fixed  $\alpha = 1, \ldots, m$ , since the boundary of  $u_{\alpha}$  has probability 0 under  $e$ , by weak convergence we have  $|\{i : e_i \in u_{\alpha}\}| / n \to \mathbb{p}[e \in u_{\alpha}]$ . then by the law of large numbers applied to the function  $f(\cdot, u_{\alpha})$ , almost surely

$$
\frac {1}{n} \sum_ {i: e _ {i} \in u _ {\alpha}} f (v _ {i}, u _ {\alpha}) \rightarrow \mathbb {p} [ e \in u _ {\alpha} ] \cdot \mathbb {e} [ f (v, u _ {\alpha}) ].
$$

summing over  $\alpha = 1,\ldots ,m$  and applying the independence of  $v$  and  $e$

$$
\begin{array}{l} \frac {1}{n} \sum_ {i: e _ {i} \in \mathcal {b}} f (v _ {i}, u (e _ {i})) \to \sum_ {\alpha = 1} ^ {m} \mathbb {p} [ e \in u _ {\alpha} ] \cdot \mathbb {e} [ f (v, u _ {\alpha}) ] \\ = \sum_ {\alpha = 1} ^ {m} \mathbb {e} [ f (v, u _ {\alpha}) \cdot \mathbf {1} \{e \in u _ {\alpha} \} ] = \mathbb {e} [ f (v, u (e)) \cdot \mathbf {1} \{e \in \mathcal {b} \} ]. \tag {e.7} \\ \end{array}
$$

combining (e.3), (e.4), (e.5), (e.6), and (e.7), we obtain

$$
\limsup _ {n \rightarrow \infty} \left| \frac {1}{n} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} f (v _ {i}, e _ {i}) - \mathbb {e} [ f (v, e) ] \right| \leq c \varepsilon
$$

for a constant  $c > 0$  independent of  $\varepsilon$ . as this holds for all  $\varepsilon > 0$ , this shows  $n^{-1} \sum_{i=1}^{n} f(v_i, e_i) \to \mathbb{e}[f(v, e)]$ , which concludes the proof.

proposition e.2. fix  $p, p' \geq 1$  and  $k, \ell \geq 1$ . if  $\mathbf{v} \in \mathbb{r}^{n \times k}$  satisfies  $\mathbf{v} \stackrel{w_{p+p'}}{\to} v$ , and  $g: \mathbb{r}^k \to \mathbb{r}^\ell$  is any continuous function satisfying  $\| g(v) \| \leq c (1 + \| v \|^{p'})$  for some  $c > 0$  and all  $v \in \mathbb{r}^k$ , then  $g(\mathbf{v}) \stackrel{w_p}{\to} g(v)$ .

proof. this follows from definition 2.1, since for any continuous function  $f: \mathbb{r}^{\ell} \to \mathbb{r}$  satisfying (2.1) for the order  $p$ , the composition  $f \circ g: \mathbb{r}^k \to \mathbb{r}$  is continuous and satisfies (2.1) for the order  $p + p'$ .

proposition e.3. fix  $p \geq 1$  and  $k \geq 0$ . suppose  $\mathbf{v} \in \mathbb{r}^{n \times k}$  satisfies  $\mathbf{v} \xrightarrow{w_p} v$ , and  $f: \mathbb{r}^k \to \mathbb{r}$  is a function satisfying (2.1) that is continuous everywhere except on a set having probability 0 under the law of  $v$ . then

$$
\frac {1}{n} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} f (\mathbf {v}) _ {i} \to \mathbb {e} [ f (v) ].
$$

proof. let  $f$  be such a function. for any  $m > 0$ , consider the bounded function  $f^{m}(v) = \max(-m, \min(f(v), m))$ . let  $v_{i}$  be the  $i^{\text{th}}$  row of  $\mathbf{v}$ . the condition  $\mathbf{v} \xrightarrow{w_{p}} v$  implies the usual weak convergence of the empirical distribution of  $\{v_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}$  to  $v$ , so  $n^{-1} \sum_{i=1}^{n} f^{m}(v_{i}) \to \mathbb{e}[f^{m}(v)]$  even when  $f^{m}$  is discontinuous on a set of probability 0 under  $v$ . now taking  $m \to \infty$ , we have  $\mathbb{e}[f^{m}(v)] \to \mathbb{e}[f(v)]$  by the bound  $|f^{m}(v)| \leq c (1 + \|v\|^{p})$  and the dominated convergence theorem. by this bound, we also have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \lim _ {m \to \infty} \lim _ {n \to \infty} \sup _ {i = 1} \frac {1}{n} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} | f ^ {m} (v _ {i}) - f (v _ {i}) | \leq \lim _ {m \to \infty} \lim _ {n \to \infty} \sup _ {i = 1} \frac {1}{n} \sum_ {i: | f (v _ {i}) | > m} | f (v _ {i}) | \\ \leq \lim _ {r \to \infty} \lim _ {n \to \infty} \sup _ {n \to \infty} \frac {1}{n} \sum_ {i: \| v _ {i} \| > r} c (1 + \| v _ {i} \| ^ {p}) = 0, \\ \end{array}
$$

where the last limit is 0 by [vil08, definition 6.7(iii)]. then  $n^{-1}\sum_{i = 1}^{n}f(v_{i})\to \mathbb{e}[f(v)]$  as desired.

proposition e.4. fix  $p \geq 1$  and  $k, \ell \geq 1$ . if  $\mathbf{v} \in \mathbb{r}^{n \times k}$ ,  $\mathbf{w} \in \mathbb{r}^{n \times \ell}$ , and  $\mathbf{m}_n, \mathbf{m} \in \mathbb{r}^{k \times \ell}$  satisfy  $\mathbf{v} \xrightarrow{w_p} (v_1, \ldots, v_k)$ ,  $\mathbf{w} \xrightarrow{w_p} 0$ , and  $\mathbf{m}_n \to \mathbf{m}$  as  $n \to \infty$ , then

$$
\mathbf {v m} _ {n} + \mathbf {w} \stackrel {{w _ {p}}} {{\to}} \left( \begin{array}{c c c} v _ {1} & \ldots & v _ {k} \end{array} \right) \mathbf {m}.
$$

proof. let  $f: \mathbb{r}^{\ell} \to \mathbb{r}$  satisfy (e.1). then  $(v_{1} \cdots v_{k}) \mapsto f((v_{1} \cdots v_{k})\mathbf{m})$  is continuous and satisfies (2.1) with the order  $p$ , so by the convergence  $\mathbf{v} \stackrel{w_{p}}{\rightarrow} (v_{1} \cdots v_{k})$ , we have

$$
\frac {1}{n} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} f (\mathbf {v m}) _ {i} \rightarrow \mathbb {e} \left[ f \left(\left(v _ {1} \dots v _ {k}\right) \mathbf {m}\right)\right].
$$

let  $v_{i}, w_{i}$  be the  $i^{\text{th}}$  rows of  $\mathbf{v}$  and  $\mathbf{w}$ . note that  $\mathbf{v} \xrightarrow{w_p} (v_1 \cdots v_k)$  implies  $n^{-1} \sum_{i=1}^{n} \|v_i\|^p \to \mathbb{e}[\|(v_1 \cdots v_k)\|^p] < \infty$ . similarly  $n^{-1} \sum_{i=1}^{n} \|w_i\|^p \to 0$ . then applying jensen's inequality, holder's inequality, and the bound  $\|v_i \mathbf{m} + w_i\|^{p-1} \leq c(\|v_i\|^{p-1} + \|w_i\|^{p-1})$ , we have for some constants  $c, c' > 0$  depending on  $\mathbf{m}$  that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \frac {1}{n} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} | f (\mathbf {v m}) _ {i} - f (\mathbf {v m} + \mathbf {w}) _ {i} | \\ \leq \frac {c}{n} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \left(1 + \| v _ {i} \mathbf {m} \| ^ {p - 1} + \| v _ {i} \mathbf {m} + w _ {i} \| ^ {p - 1}\right) \| w _ {i} \| \\ \leq c ^ {\prime} \cdot \frac {1}{n} \left(\| w _ {i} \| + \| v _ {i} \| ^ {p - 1} \| w _ {i} \| + \| w _ {i} \| ^ {p}\right) \\ \leq c ^ {\prime} \left[ \left(\frac {1}{n} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \| w _ {i} \| ^ {p}\right) ^ {1 / p} + \left(\frac {1}{n} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \| v _ {i} \| ^ {p}\right) ^ {(p - 1) / p} \left(\frac {1}{n} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \| w _ {i} \| ^ {p}\right) ^ {1 / p} + \left(\frac {1}{n} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \| w _ {i} \| ^ {p}\right) \right] \to 0. \\ \end{array}
$$

similarly,

$$
\begin{array}{l} \frac {1}{n} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} | f (\mathbf {v m} _ {n} + \mathbf {w}) _ {i} - f (\mathbf {v m} + \mathbf {w}) _ {i} | \\ \leq \frac {c}{n} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \left(1 + \| v _ {i} \mathbf {m} _ {n} + w _ {i} \| ^ {p - 1} + \| v _ {i} \mathbf {m} + w _ {i} \| ^ {p - 1}\right) \cdot \| v _ {i} (\mathbf {m} _ {n} - \mathbf {m}) \| \\ \leq c ^ {\prime} \| \mathbf {m} _ {n} - \mathbf {m} \| \cdot \frac {1}{n} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} (\| v _ {i} \| + \| v _ {i} \| ^ {p} + \| w _ {i} \| ^ {p - 1} \| v _ {i} \|) \to 0. \\ \end{array}
$$

combining the above yields the proposition.

the following is an empirical form of stein's lemma.

proposition e.5. fix  $p \geq 2$ . suppose  $(\mathbf{z}_1, \ldots, \mathbf{z}_t, \mathbf{e}) \in \mathbb{r}^{n \times (t + k)}$  are such that

$$
\left(\mathbf {z} _ {1}, \dots , \mathbf {z} _ {t}, \mathbf {e}\right) \stackrel {{w _ {p}}} {{\rightarrow}} \left(z _ {1}, \dots , z _ {t}, e\right)
$$

where, for some non-singular covariance matrix  $\sigma \in \mathbb{r}^{t\times t}$ ,  $(z_{1},\ldots ,z_{t})\sim \mathcal{n}(0,\sigma)$  and this is independent of  $e$ . suppose  $u:\mathbb{r}^{t + k}\to \mathbb{r}$  is weakly differentiable in its first  $t$  arguments and satisfies (2.1) for the order  $p - 1$ . then, almost surely as  $n\to \infty$

$$
\frac {1}{n} \left( \begin{array}{c} \mathbf {z} _ {1} ^ {\top} \\ \vdots \\ \mathbf {z} _ {t} ^ {\top} \end{array} \right) u (\mathbf {z} _ {1}, \ldots , \mathbf {z} _ {t}, \mathbf {e}) \to \sigma \cdot \left( \begin{array}{c} \mathbb {e} [ \partial_ {1} u (z _ {1}, \ldots , z _ {t}, e) ] \\ \vdots \\ \mathbb {e} [ \partial_ {t} u (z _ {1}, \ldots , z _ {t}, e) ] \end{array} \right).
$$

proof. note that for each  $s = 1, \ldots, t$ , the function  $(z_1, \ldots, z_t, e) \mapsto z_s u(z_1, \ldots, z_t, e)$  is continuous and satisfies (2.1) with order  $p$ , so

$$
\frac {1}{n} \left(\mathbf {z} _ {1}, \dots , \mathbf {z} _ {t}\right) ^ {\top} u \left(\mathbf {z} _ {1}, \dots , \mathbf {z} _ {t}, \mathbf {e}\right)\rightarrow \mathbb {e} \left[\left(z _ {1}, \dots , z _ {t}\right) \cdot u \left(z _ {1}, \dots , z _ {t}, e\right)\right]. \tag {e.8}
$$

to show that the right side of (e.8) is equivalent to the given expression, we apply stein's lemma: let us condition on a realization  $e = e$  for any fixed  $e \in \mathbb{r}^k$ , and denote  $z = (z_1, \ldots, z_t)$ . we may write  $z = \sigma^{1/2} x$  where  $x \sim \mathcal{n}(0, \mathrm{id})$ , and define

$$
v _ {e} (x) = u (\sigma^ {1 / 2} x, e).
$$

since  $\sigma$  is non-singular, the maps  $x\mapsto \sigma^{1 / 2}x$  and  $z\mapsto \sigma^{-1 / 2}z$  are both lipschitz. then by the chain rule for weak differentiability under bi-lipschitzian changes of coordinates, see [zie12, theorem 2.2.2],  $v_{e}(x)$  is weakly differentiable with

$$
\nabla v _ {e} (x) = \sigma^ {1 / 2} \cdot \nabla u (\sigma^ {1 / 2} x, e)
$$

a.e. over  $x \in \mathbb{r}^t$ . (we denote by  $\nabla(\cdot)$  the vector of partial derivatives.) applying stein's lemma for weakly differentiable functions, see [fsw18, theorem 2.1], we have for each  $s = 1, \ldots, t$  that  $\mathbb{e}[x_s v_e(x)] = \mathbb{e}[\partial_s v_e(x)]$ . then

$$
\mathbb {e} [ z \cdot u (z, e) ] = \mathbb {e} [ \sigma^ {1 / 2} x \cdot v _ {e} (x) ] = \sigma^ {1 / 2} \cdot \mathbb {e} [ \nabla v _ {e} (x) ] = \sigma \cdot \mathbb {e} [ \nabla u (z, e) ].
$$

taking the expectation over  $e$  and applying this to (e.8) concludes the proof.

# appendix f. auxiliary lemmas

this appendix collects several auxiliary results that were used in the preceding arguments.

f.1. properties of haar-orthogonal matrices. the following result was established as [rsf19, lemma 4].

proposition f.1. fix  $k \geq 1$ , and let  $\mathbf{x}, \mathbf{y} \in \mathbb{r}^{n \times k}$  be deterministic matrices with rank  $k$ , such that  $\mathbf{x} = \mathbf{q}\mathbf{y}$  for some orthogonal matrix  $\mathbf{q} \in \mathbb{r}^{n \times n}$ . if  $\mathbf{o} \in \mathbb{r}^{n \times n}$  is a random haar-uniform orthogonal matrix, then the law of  $\mathbf{o}$  conditioned on  $\mathbf{x} = \mathbf{o}\mathbf{y}$  is equal to the law of

$$
\mathbf {x} \left(\mathbf {x} ^ {\top} \mathbf {x}\right) ^ {- 1} \mathbf {y} ^ {\top} + \pi_ {\mathbf {x} ^ {\bot}} \tilde {\mathbf {o}} \pi_ {\mathbf {y} ^ {\bot}} ^ {\top} = \mathbf {x} \left(\mathbf {y} ^ {\top} \mathbf {y}\right) ^ {- 1} \mathbf {y} ^ {\top} + \pi_ {\mathbf {x} ^ {\bot}} \tilde {\mathbf {o}} \pi_ {\mathbf {y} ^ {\bot}} ^ {\top}.
$$

here,  $\tilde{\mathbf{o}}\in \mathbb{r}^{(n - k)\times (n - k)}$  is a haar-uniform orthogonal matrix, and  $\pi_{\mathbf{x}^{\perp}},\pi_{\mathbf{y}^{\perp}}\in \mathbb{r}^{n\times (n - k)}$  are matrices with orthonormal columns spanning the orthogonal complements of the column spans of  $\mathbf{x}$  and  $\mathbf{y}$ .

proposition f.2. fix any  $p \geq 1$  and  $k, \ell \geq 0$ . let  $\mathbf{o} \in \mathbb{r}^{(n - \ell) \times (n - \ell)}$  be a random haar-uniform orthogonal matrix. let  $\mathbf{e} \in \mathbb{r}^{n \times k}$  and  $\mathbf{v} \in \mathbb{r}^{n - \ell}$  be deterministic and satisfy  $\mathbf{e} \xrightarrow{w_p} e$  and  $n^{-1} \| \mathbf{v} \|^2 \to \sigma^2$ , and let  $\pi \in \mathbb{r}^{n \times (n - \ell)}$  be any deterministic matrix with orthonormal columns.

(a) almost surely as  $n\to \infty$

$$
(\pi \mathbf {o v}, \mathbf {e}) \stackrel {w _ {p}} {\rightarrow} (z, e)
$$

where  $z\sim \mathcal{n}(0,\sigma^2)$  is independent of  $e$

(b) consider a second dimension  $m$  such that  $m, n \to \infty$  simultaneously. fix  $j \geq 0$ , and let  $\mathbf{f} \in \mathbb{r}^{m \times j}$  be deterministic and satisfy  $\mathbf{f} \xrightarrow{w_p} f$ . let  $\check{\mathbf{v}} \in \mathbb{r}^m$  be the first  $m$  entries of  $\pi \mathbf{o}\mathbf{v}$  if  $m \leq n$ , or  $\pi \mathbf{o}\mathbf{v}$  extended by  $m - n$  i.i.d. entries with distribution  $\mathcal{n}(0, \sigma^2)$  if  $m > n$ . then almost surely as  $m, n \to \infty$ ,

$$
(\check {\mathbf {v}}, \mathbf {f}) \stackrel {w _ {p}} {\rightarrow} (\check {z}, f)
$$

where  $\check{z} \sim \mathcal{n}(0, \sigma^2)$  is independent of  $f$ .

proof. for part (a), observe that  $\mathbf{o}\mathbf{v}$  is a random vector uniformly distributed on the sphere of radius  $\| \mathbf{v} \|$ . thus, we may introduce a gaussian vector  $\mathbf{z} \sim \mathcal{n}(0, \mathrm{id}_{n \times n})$  so that  $\pi^{\top}\mathbf{z} \sim \mathcal{n}(0, \mathrm{id}_{(n - \ell) \times (n - \ell)})$ , and write  $\mathbf{o}\mathbf{v} = \pi^{\top}\mathbf{z} \cdot \| \mathbf{v} \| / \| \pi^{\top}\mathbf{z} \|$ . then

$$
\pi \mathbf {o} \mathbf {v} = \pi \pi^ {\top} \mathbf {z} \cdot \| \mathbf {v} \| / \| \pi^ {\top} \mathbf {z} \| = \mathbf {z} \cdot \| \mathbf {v} \| / \| \pi^ {\top} \mathbf {z} \| - \pi^ {\perp} \mathbf {z} \cdot \| \mathbf {v} \| / \| \pi^ {\top} \mathbf {z} \| \tag {f.1}
$$

where  $\pi^{\perp} = \mathrm{id} - \pi \pi^{\top}\in \mathbb{r}^{n\times n}$  is a projection onto a subspace of fixed dimension  $\ell$ . by proposition e.1,

$$
(\mathbf {z}, \mathbf {e}) \stackrel {w _ {p}} {\rightarrow} (\tilde {z}, e) \tag {f.2}
$$

where  $\tilde{z} \sim \mathcal{n}(0,1)$ . we have  $n^{-1}\| \mathbf{v}\|^2 \to \sigma^2$  by assumption and  $n^{-1}\| \pi^\top \mathbf{z}\|^2 \to 1$  almost surely, so

$$
\| \mathbf {v} \| / \| \pi^ {\top} \mathbf {z} \| \rightarrow \sigma . \tag {f.3}
$$

we also have the equality in law  $\pi^{\perp}\mathbf{z} = \mathbf{u}_1w_1 + \ldots +\mathbf{u}_\ell w_\ell$  for some orthonormal unit vectors  $\mathbf{u}_1,\dots ,\mathbf{u}_\ell \in \mathbb{r}^n$  spanning the range of  $\pi^{\perp}$ , and for  $w_{1},\ldots ,w_{\ell}\stackrel {iid}{\sim}\mathcal{n}(0,1)$ . letting  $\{u_{ij}\}_{i = 1}^{n}$  be the entries of  $\mathbf{u}_j$ , for each  $j = 1,\dots ,\ell$  and any fixed  $p\geq 1$ , we have

$$
\frac {1}{n} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} | u _ {i j} w _ {j} | ^ {p} \leq | w _ {j} | ^ {p} \cdot \frac {1}{\sqrt {n}} \rightarrow 0
$$

almost surely as  $n\to \infty$  . thus also

$$
\frac {1}{n} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \left| (\pi^ {\perp} \mathbf {z}) _ {i} \right| ^ {p} \rightarrow 0,
$$

so  $\pi^{\perp}\mathbf{z} \cdot \| \mathbf{v}\| /\| \pi^{\top}\mathbf{z}\| \stackrel{w_{p}}{\to} 0$ . combining this with (f.1), (f.2), and (f.3) and applying proposition e.4, we obtain part (a).

for part (b), let  $\breve{\mathbf{z}}\in \mathbb{r}^m$  be the first  $m$  entries of  $\mathbf{z}$  if  $m\leq n$ , or  $\mathbf{z}$  extended by  $m - n$  additional  $\mathcal{n}(0,1)$  entries if  $m > n$ . let  $\mathbf{r}_1\in \mathbb{r}^m$  be the first  $m$  entries of  $\pi^{\perp}\mathbf{z}\cdot \| \mathbf{v}\| /\| \pi^{\top}\mathbf{z}\|$  if  $m\leq n$ , or this vector extended by  $m - n$  additional 0's if  $m > n$ . let  $\mathbf{r}_2\in \mathbb{r}^m$  be 0 if  $m\leq n$ , or equal to 0 in the first  $n$  entries and equal to  $\breve{\mathbf{z}}\cdot (\| \mathbf{v}\| /\| \pi^{\top}\mathbf{z}\| -\sigma)$  in the last  $m - n$  entries if  $m > n$ . then we may write

$$
\check {\mathbf {v}} = \check {\mathbf {z}} \cdot \left\| \mathbf {v} \right\| / \left\| \pi^ {\top} \mathbf {z} \right\| - \mathbf {r} _ {1} - \mathbf {r} _ {2}.
$$

the same argument as in part (a) shows

$$
(\breve {\mathbf {z}}, \mathbf {f}) \stackrel {w _ {p}} {\rightarrow} (\tilde {z}, f)
$$

where  $\tilde{z} \sim \mathcal{n}(0,1)$  is independent of  $f$ , and  $\mathbf{r}_1 \xrightarrow{w_p} 0$ . when  $m > n$ , we also have

$$
\frac {1}{m} \sum_ {i = n + 1} ^ {m} \left|\left(\tilde {\mathbf {z}} \left(\| \mathbf {v} \| / \| \pi^ {\top} \mathbf {z} \| - \sigma\right)\right) _ {i} \right| ^ {p} \leq \left| \| \mathbf {v} \| / \| \pi^ {\top} \mathbf {z} \| - \sigma \right| ^ {p} \cdot \frac {1}{m} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {m} | (\tilde {\mathbf {z}}) _ {i} | ^ {p} \rightarrow 0
$$

almost surely. so  $\mathbf{r}_2 \stackrel{w_p}{\to} 0$ . then applying proposition e.4 shows part (b).

# f.2. properties of moments and free cumulants.

proposition f.3. let  $\lambda$  be a random variable with finite moments of all orders, such that  $\mathbb{e}[|\lambda|^k] \leq m^k$  for some  $m > 0$  and all integers  $k \geq 1$ .

(a) let  $\{\kappa_k\}_{k\geq 1}$  be the free cumulants of  $\lambda$ . then for all  $k\geq 1$

$$
\left| \kappa_ {k} \right| \leq (1 6 m) ^ {k}.
$$

thus the  $r$ -transform of  $\lambda$  is analytic on the domain  $|x| < 1/(16m)$ , where it may be defined by the convergent series

$$
r (x) = \sum_ {k = 1} ^ {\infty} \kappa_ {k} x ^ {k - 1}.
$$

(b) let  $\{\kappa_{2k}\}_{k\geq 1}$  be the rectangular free cumulants of  $\lambda$  with aspect ratio  $\gamma$ . then for all  $k\geq 1$

$$
\left| \kappa_ {2 k} \right| \leq \max  \left(\gamma^ {k}, 1\right) \cdot (1 6 m) ^ {2 k}.
$$

thus the rectangular  $r$ -transform of  $\lambda$  is analytic on the domain  $|x| < \min(\gamma^{-1}, 1) / (16m)^2$ , where it may be defined by the convergent series

$$
r (x) = \sum_ {k = 1} ^ {\infty} \kappa_ {2 k} x ^ {k}.
$$

proof. for part (a), the free cumulants may be expressed explicitly by möbius inversion of the moment-cumulant relations (2.3), yielding

$$
\kappa_ {k} = \sum_ {\pi \in \mathrm {n c} (k)} m _ {\pi} \cdot \mu (\pi , 1 _ {k}), \qquad m _ {\pi} = \prod_ {s \in \pi} m _ {| s |},
$$

where  $\mu (\cdot ,\cdot)$  are the möbius functions on the non-crossing partition lattice and  $1_{k}$  is the trivial partition consisting of the single set  $\{1,\ldots ,k\}$ . we have  $|\mu (\pi ,1_k)|\leq 4^k$  and  $|\mathrm{nc}(k)|\leq 4^{k}$  -see the proof of [ns06, proposition 13.15]. combining with  $|m_{\pi}|\leq m^{k}$  for all  $\pi \in \mathrm{nc}(k)$ , part (a) follows.

for part (b), we apply a similar argument in the rectangular probability space  $(\mathcal{a}, p_m, p_n, \varphi_m, \varphi_n)$  from which the rectangular free cumulants are defined—see [bg09b, section 1.2] for definitions. here,  $p_m, p_n \in \mathcal{a}$  are orthogonal projections satisfying  $p_m + p_n = 1$ , and  $\varphi_m$  and  $\varphi_n$  are traces on  $p_m \mathcal{a} p_m$  and  $p_n \mathcal{a} p_n$  that satisfy  $\varphi_m(p_m) = 1$ ,  $\varphi_n(p_n) = 1$ , and  $\gamma/(1+\gamma) \cdot \varphi_m(xy) = 1/(1+\gamma) \cdot \varphi_n(yx)$  for  $x \in p_m \mathcal{a} p_n$  and  $y \in p_n \mathcal{a} p_m$ . let  $e: \mathcal{a} \to \mathcal{d}$  be the conditional expectation onto the sub-algebra  $\mathcal{d}$  generated by  $(p_m, p_n)$ , given by  $e(x) = \varphi_m(p_m x p_m) p_m + \varphi_n(p_n x p_n) p_n$ . for  $k \geq 1$  and partitions  $\pi \in \mathrm{nc}(k)$ , let  $\kappa_{\pi}^{\mathcal{d}}$  be the  $\mathcal{d}$ -valued free cumulants defined by the moment-cumulant relations

$$
e (a _ {1} \dots a _ {k}) = \sum_ {\pi \in \mathrm {n c} (k)} \kappa_ {\pi} ^ {\mathcal {d}} (a _ {1}, \dots , a _ {k}).
$$

if  $a \in p_m\mathcal{a}p_n$  is an element such that  $\varphi_m((aa^*)^k) = \mathbb{e}[\lambda^{2k}]$ , then the rectangular free cumulant  $\kappa_{2k}$  of  $\lambda$  is given by

$$
\kappa_ {2 k} \cdot p _ {m} = \kappa_ {1 _ {2 k}} ^ {\mathcal {d}} (a, a ^ {*}, \ldots , a, a ^ {*}).
$$

(compare [bg09a, eq. (2.5)] with [bg09b, eq. (8)], the latter being the definition of rectangular free cumulants that we have reviewed in section 2.4 and used throughout this work.) from the möbius inversion

$$
\kappa_ {1 _ {2 k}} ^ {\mathcal {d}} (a, a ^ {*}, \ldots , a, a ^ {*}) = \sum_ {\pi \in \mathrm {n c} (2 k)} m _ {\pi} ^ {\mathcal {d}} (a, a ^ {*}, \ldots , a, a ^ {*}) \cdot \mu (\pi , 1 _ {2 k})
$$

where  $m_{\pi}^{\mathcal{d}}$  is the  $\mathcal{d}$ -valued joint moment function associated to  $\pi$ , we obtain

$$
\left| \kappa_ {2 k} \right| = \left| \varphi_ {m} \big (\kappa_ {1 _ {2 k}} ^ {\mathcal {d}} (a, a ^ {*}, \ldots , a, a ^ {*}) \big) \right| \leq 1 6 ^ {2 k} \max _ {\pi \in \mathrm {n c} (2 k)} \left| \varphi_ {m} (m _ {\pi} ^ {\mathcal {d}} (a, a ^ {*}, \ldots , a, a ^ {*})) \right|.
$$

here, it may be checked that when  $\varphi_{m}(m_{\pi}^{\mathcal{d}}(a,a^{*},\dots ,a,a^{*}))$  is non-zero, it must be a product of  $\varphi_{m}((aa^{*})^{i_{1}}),\ldots ,\varphi_{m}((aa^{*})^{i_{a}})$  and  $\varphi_{n}((a^{*}a)^{j_{1}}),\ldots ,\varphi_{n}((a^{*}a)^{j_{b}})$  where the elements of  $\pi$  have cardinalities  $2i_{1},\ldots ,2i_{a},2j_{1},\ldots ,2j_{b}$ . then applying  $\varphi_{n}((a^{*}a)^{j}) = \gamma \varphi_{m}((aa^{*})^{j})$  and  $\varphi_{m}((aa^{*})^{j}) = \mathbb{e}[\lambda^{2j}]\leq m^{2j}$ , we obtain  $|\varphi_{m}(m_{\pi}^{\mathcal{d}}(a,a^{*},\dots ,a,a^{*}))|\leq \max (\gamma^{k},1)m^{2k}$ , which yields part (b).

proposition f.4. let  $k$  be a positive integer. then for any random variable  $x$  and any sigma-algebra  $\mathcal{f}$ ,

$$
\mathbb {e} \left[ \left(x - \mathbb {e} [ x \mid \mathcal {f} ]\right) ^ {k} \right] \leq 2 ^ {k} \mathbb {e} \left[ | x | ^ {k} \right].
$$

proof. write as shorthand  $y = \mathbb{e}[x \mid \mathcal{f}]$ . we expand the left side and apply hölder's inequality to obtain

$$
\mathbb{e}[(x - y)^{k}] = \sum_{j = 0}^{k}\binom {k}{j}\mathbb{e}[x^{j}y^{k - j}]\leq \sum_{j = 0}^{k}\binom {k}{j}\mathbb{e}[|x|^{k}]^{j / k}\mathbb{e}[|y|^{k}]^{(k - j) / k}.
$$

by jensen's inequality,

$$
\mathbb {e} [ | y | ^ {k} ] = \mathbb {e} \left[ | \mathbb {e} [ x \mid \mathcal {f} ] | ^ {k} \right] \leq \mathbb {e} \left[ \mathbb {e} [ | x | ^ {k} \mid \mathcal {f} ] \right] = \mathbb {e} [ | x | ^ {k} ],
$$

and the result follows from  $\sum_{j=0}^{k} \binom{k}{j} = 2^k$ .
